613	\midrule
614	WikiText-103 & 8.82 & 40.78 & 68.59 & 40.6 & 7.35 & 70.85 / 74.81 \\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_uniform_hellaswag_piqa
615	C4 & 11.55 & 43.09 & 68.67 & 39.95 & 4.09 & 69.24 / 75.03 \\ % exp_id: c4_kl30_s42_eval

619	
620	The C4-trained predictor achieves comparable MMLU (40.0\% vs.\ 40.6\%) but worse GSM8K (4.1\% vs.\ 7.4\%) and higher PPL (11.55 vs.\ 8.82), despite training on more diverse text.
621	This rules out training data composition as the primary cause of knowledge-task regression and supports the hypothesis that forward KL's mode-covering property structurally under-protects rare reasoning-critical activations. % exp_id: c4_control_eval

only the top-$k$ threshold changes.\n\nNote that the current implementation uses unstructured element-wise masks, which do not translate to wall-clock speedup without sparse kernel support.\nThe pipeline adds $\\sim$6.4\\% overhead; net acceleration requires kernel-level sparse computation optimizations (e.g., Triton block-sparse, cuSPARSE) that are beyond the scope of this work.\nOur contribution is prediction quality and inference simplification (removing the compensation network), not end-to-end latency reduction.\n\n\n\\section{Predictor Bottleneck Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:db_sensitivity}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:db_sensitivity} reports the effect of predictor bottleneck dimension $d_b$ on PPL at 50\\% sparsity under both TEAL global allocation (the paper's primary inference protocol) and per-token evaluation.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Predictor bottleneck sensitivity at 50\\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$). TEAL global is the primary inference protocol; per-token shown for reference.}\n\\label{tab:db_sensitivity}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n$d_b$ & Params & TEAL global & $\\Delta$ & Per-token \\\\\n\\midrule\n64 & 38.2M (0.48\\%) & 12.076 & +0.9\\% & 10.16 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n128 & 75.9M (0.95\\%) & 11.963 & --- & 9.218 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n256 & 151.5M (1.89\\%) & 12.105 & +1.2\\% & 8.586 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n512 & 302.5M (3.77\\%) & 12.315 & +2.9\\% & 7.93 \\\\ % exp_id: db_sensitivity_sweep\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder TEAL global allocation, increasing $d_b$ beyond 128 provides no benefit (PPL slightly worsens), while per-token evaluation shows monotonic improvement (10.16$\\to$7.93).\nThis divergence indicates that the global top-$k$ allocation mechanism, not predictor capacity, is the binding constraint: additional predictor capacity produces better per-neuron scores, but the global ranking and budget distribution cannot exploit them.\nThe $d_b{=}128$ configuration (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base model) is therefore optimal for the TEAL inference protocol.\n\n\\subsection{Temperature Schedule Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:tau_sensitivity}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temperature schedule sensitivity at 50\\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$, TEAL global).}\n\\label{tab:tau_sensitivity}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lc}\n\\toprule\n$\\tau$ Schedule & PPL$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFixed $\\tau{=}0.5$ & \\textbf{11.861} \\\\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep\nLinear $1.0 \\to 0.1$ (default) & 11.963 \\\\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep\nFixed $\\tau{=}0.1$ & 12.072 \\\\ % exp_id: tau_schedule_sweep\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTemperature schedule has minimal impact ($<$1.8\\% PPL variation).\nThe linear annealing default is a conservative choice; even removing annealing entirely (fixed $\\tau{=}0.5$) yields comparable performance.\n\n\\subsection{Training Convergence}\n\\label{app:training_convergence}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Training convergence at 50\\% sparsity (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$, TEAL global).}\n\\label{tab:training_convergence}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{cc}\n\\toprule\nSteps & PPL$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n200 & 15.158 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n400 & 12.538 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n600 & 12.046 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n800 & 11.907 \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n1000 & \\textbf{11.906} \\\\ % exp_id: training_steps_sweep\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTraining converges rapidly: 95\\% of the quality gap closes by step 600, and performance saturates by step 800 ($\\Delta < 0.001$ between 800 and 1000 steps).\nOur default of 1000 steps provides a conservative margin.\n\n\n\\section{Geometric Penalty Schedule Dynamics}\n\\label{app:lagrangian}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:lagrangian} reports the convergence of the geometric penalty schedule across sparsity targets.\nThe geometric doubling pattern ($1 \\to 2 \\to 4 \\to 8 \\to \\ldots$) enables $\\lambda$ to reach steady state within $\\sim$10 steps; the deadzone ($\\epsilon = 0.02$) then stabilizes $\\lambda$ once actual sparsity is within $\\pm$2\\% of the target.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Geometric penalty schedule convergence across sparsity targets (margin $\\epsilon=0.02$, 1000 training steps). Deviation is $|$actual $-$ target$|$ / target.}\n\\label{tab:lagrangian}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nTarget $\\rho$ & $\\lambda_{\\max}$ & Final $\\lambda$ & Actual sparsity & Deviation \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & 5000 & 5000 (cap) & 0.285 & 5.0\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_30pct_s42\n40\\% & 5000 & 625 & 0.398 & 0.5\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_40pct_s42\n50\\% & 1000 & 8 & 0.504 & 0.8\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_50pct_s42\n70\\% & 1000 & 1000 (cap) & 0.688 & 1.7\\% \\\\ % exp_id: kl_70pct_s42\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 50\\% sparsity, the SwiGLU gate distribution already clusters near the target, so $\\lambda$ remains low (8); the constraint is nearly satisfied by the initial predictor outputs.\nAt 30\\% and 70\\%, the gap between the gate prior and target is larger, driving $\\lambda$ to its respective caps.\nThe 40\\% target converges to $\\lambda = 625$ with only 0.5\\% deviation; this precision reflects a regime where the target is reachable without $\\lambda$ saturation.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{lagrangian_dynamics.pdf}\n\\caption{Lagrangian controller dynamics for 30\\% sparsity target ($\\lambda_{\\max}=5000$). Left axis (log scale): penalty weight $\\lambda$ rises from $0.01$ to its cap once actual sparsity undershoots the target at $\\sim$step~150, exhibiting bang-bang oscillations before saturating. Right axis: actual sparsity (solid) converges from the natural density ($\\approx$0.41) toward the target (dashed) and stabilizes near 0.28.}\n\\label{fig:lagrangian_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTemperature $\\tau$ is annealed linearly from 1.0 to 0.1 over 1000 steps.\nA mild loss increase in the final $\\sim$100 steps is expected: as masks become more binary ($\\tau \\to 0$), discrete rounding noise slightly increases KL divergence, consistent with convergence toward the hard-mask inference regime.\n\nThe controller reliably drives actual sparsity to within 5\\% of the target across all tested regimes, with $\\lambda$ spanning three orders of magnitude (8 to 5000) via geometric doubling.\nFor the 30\\% target, actual sparsity is $\\approx$28.5\\% (5\\% deviation). This does not affect conclusions: TEAL at 28.5\\% actual sparsity yields PPL 10.30 (vs.\\ 10.97 at exact 30\\%), a 0.67 PPL difference negligible relative to the KL vs.\\ TEAL gap (8.96 vs.\\ 10.97).\n\n\n\\section{Out-of-Distribution Evaluation}\n\\label{app:ood}\n\nTo address concerns about data homogeneity between training (WikiText-103) and evaluation (WikiText-2), we evaluate on C4~\\cite{raffel2020c4} validation set under TEAL global allocation.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Out-of-distribution evaluation on C4 validation (PPL$\\downarrow$). 30\\% KL reports 2-seed mean$\\pm$std; other entries use seed 42.}\n\\label{tab:ood}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Method & C4 PPL$\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% & Dense & 9.21 \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & KL predictor (Ours) & \\textbf{16.03$\\pm$0.01} \\\\ % exp_id: c4_ood_kl30_s42, c4_ood_kl30_s123\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{50\\%} & KL predictor (Ours) & \\textbf{18.40} \\\\ % exp_id: eval_c4_ood_kl50\n& TEAL vanilla & 38.75 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt the 30\\% sparsity level, the KL predictor achieves C4 PPL 16.03$\\pm$0.01 (2-seed, global allocation, $1.74\\times$ dense), confirming OOD generalization.\nAt 50\\%, the KL predictor achieves 52.5\\% relative advantage over TEAL (18.40 vs.\\ 38.75), comparable to the 50\\% advantage on WikiText-2 (11.92 vs.\\ 23.90), confirming that KL-trained predictions generalize across distributions.\n\n\\paragraph{Uniform Allocation.}\nUnder uniform allocation at 30\\%, C4 PPL across three seeds is 18.22 (s42), 16.37 (s123), 16.36 (s456), yielding mean 16.98$\\pm$1.07 ($1.84\\times$ dense). % exp_id: c4_ood_kl30_uniform_3seed\nThe cross-seed variance is substantially higher than in-domain: CV=6.3\\% on C4 vs.\\ 0.2\\% on WikiText-2 (PPL 8.81$\\pm$0.02), driven by seed 42's elevated C4 PPL (18.22 vs.\\ 16.37 for other seeds).\nGlobal allocation's cross-layer budget flexibility partially compensates for domain shift (PPL 16.03 global vs.\\ 16.98 uniform), consistent with the Limitations observation that the predictor partially overfits to training-domain activation patterns.\n\n\\paragraph{Downstream Task Robustness.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:ood_downstream} compares downstream performance when the predictor generates masks from C4 text (OOD) vs.\\ WikiText (in-domain), both at 30\\% uniform allocation (seed 42). All six benchmarks show $\\Delta$$\\leq$1.1pp, confirming that predictor masks are robust to calibration domain shift. % exp_id: c4_ood_downstream_kl30, bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_uniform_hellaswag_piqa\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Downstream task robustness to calibration domain shift (KL 30\\% uniform, seed 42). WikiText: predictor masks from in-domain text; C4: masks from OOD text. Metrics: acc\\_norm (ARC-c, HS, PIQA), acc (WG, MMLU), exact\\_match (GSM8K).}\n\\label{tab:ood_downstream}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nCalibration & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & HS & PIQA \\\\\n\\midrule\nWikiText & 40.8 & 68.6 & 40.6 & 7.4 & 70.9 & 74.8 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_uniform_hellaswag_piqa\nC4 (OOD) & 40.4 & 69.3 & 41.4 & 6.3 & 70.7 & 74.0 \\\\ % exp_id: c4_ood_downstream_kl30\n\\midrule\n$\\Delta$ & $-$0.4 & +0.7 & +0.8 & $-$1.1 & $-$0.2 & $-$0.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Retention Analysis}\n\\label{app:retention}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{per_layer_retention_chart.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer neuron retention under global top-$k$ allocation at 30\\%, 50\\%, and 70\\% sparsity. Dashed lines indicate uniform retention baselines. Late layers (L24--31) are consistently starved due to long-tailed activation distributions, with retention dropping to 28.4\\% (L31) even at 30\\% sparsity. At 70\\%, starvation extends to mid-layers (L10--12: 16.7--20.1\\%), explaining the transition to the capacity-limited regime.}\n\\label{fig:retention}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Training Stability}\n\\label{app:stability}\n\nThree-seed evaluation ($s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$) at 50\\% sparsity under uniform allocation yields PPL $11.92 \\pm 0.07$ (CV=0.6\\%); training is stable across random initializations. % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\nDownstream metrics are similarly stable: ARC-c $33.8 \\pm 1.2$\\%, WinoGrande $62.9 \\pm 0.1$\\%, MMLU $28.0 \\pm 0.6$\\%, GSM8K $2.2 \\pm 0.1$\\%.\n\nUnder global allocation at 50\\%, MMLU is $28.3 \\pm 0.7$\\%; the near-random performance is capacity-limited rather than seed-sensitive ($\\sigma$=0.7pp vs.\\ 9.5pp at 30\\% global). % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, kl50_global_s123_downstream_v2, kl50_global_s456_eval\nThe low standard deviation across both allocation strategies indicates that the KL training landscape is well-conditioned for this predictor architecture, with low sensitivity to initialization.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Seed Mask Consistency}\n\\label{app:cross_seed_jaccard}\n\nTo assess whether KL predictors discover consistent mask patterns across initializations, we compute pairwise Jaccard distances among three independently trained KL predictors (s42/s123/s456) at 30\\% uniform allocation (100 sequences, WikiText-2 validation). % exp_id: cross_seed_jaccard_kl30\n\nThe mean cross-seed Jaccard distance is 0.363 (IoU = 0.637), substantially lower than the KL-vs-magnitude divergence (0.46--0.68), confirming that KL-discovered patterns form a distinct cluster from magnitude-based masks.\nPer-layer divergence follows a U-shaped pattern: lowest at embedding-adjacent layers (L0: 0.33), peaking at early layers (L1: 0.41), with a mid-network valley (L12--14: 0.33). The random baseline Jaccard distance is 0.462.\n\nWhile specific mask configurations vary moderately across seeds (Jaccard distance 36.3\\%), the resulting PPL is highly stable (8.81$\\pm$0.02, CV=0.2\\%), suggesting multiple near-equivalent optima in the activation mask space.\nThe cross-seed divergence (36.3\\%) is substantially lower than the KL-vs-magnitude divergence (46--68\\%), confirming that KL discovers effective patterns that cluster distinctly from magnitude-based selection.\n\n\n\\section{MSE Baseline Configuration}\n\\label{app:mse_config}\n\nThe MSE predictor uses identical architecture (two-layer MLP, bottleneck $d_b{=}128$), training data (WikiText-103, 1000 steps), optimizer (AdamW, lr=$1{\\times}10^{-3}$, weight decay $1{\\times}10^{-2}$, cosine decay), Gumbel-Sigmoid mask generation ($\\tau$: $1.0 \\to 0.1$), and adaptive Lagrangian sparsity control as the KL and BCE predictors.\nThe only difference is the loss target: per-layer $\\|\\mathbf{W}_d^{(\\ell)}(\\tilde{\\mathbf{m}}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{g}^{(\\ell)} \\odot \\mathbf{x}^{(\\ell)} \\mathbf{W}_u^{(\\ell)}) - \\mathbf{y}_{\\text{dense}}^{(\\ell)}\\|_2^2$, replacing the end-to-end KL divergence.\nThis controlled comparison isolates the effect of output-distribution alignment: all three predictors see identical data in identical order with identical architectures; only the loss function differs. % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42\n\nMSE uses $\\lambda_{\\max}{=}1000$ (vs.\\ KL's 5000) because MSE's per-layer gradient magnitude is approximately $5\\times$ larger than KL's end-to-end gradient, requiring proportionally less penalty pressure to achieve comparable sparsity control. Both reach their target sparsity within 110 steps.\nAt 50\\% target, MSE converges to the target sparsity for seed 42; however, with seed 123, sparsity reaches only 5.1\\% under the same $\\lambda_{\\max}$ cap (eval PPL$=$15{,}789, random-level), indicating high sensitivity to initialization---a failure mode not observed with KL-based losses.\nAt 30\\%, MSE achieves 27.7\\% actual sparsity for s42 (gap 2.3pp).\nThe 3-seed MSE mean (18.52$\\pm$1.68; s42=20.43, s123=17.73, s456=17.39) is still $1.7\\times$ worse than TEAL (10.82), confirming that the performance deficit stems from the loss function, not insufficient sparsity pressure.\n\n\n\\section{Extended SNR Analysis}\n\\label{app:snr_extended}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:snr_trajectory} plots gradient SNR across the full 1000-step training for both KL and BCE predictors, measured at 14 checkpoints (LLaMA-3.1-8B, C4) with 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals.\nFrom step 100 onward, BCE maintains consistently higher SNR than KL (2.3--17$\\times$ depending on training phase), with the gap narrowing toward convergence ($2.3\\times$ at step 1000: $2.026{\\times}10^{-3}$ vs.\\ $8.78{\\times}10^{-4}$). % exp_id: snr_full_curve_1000steps\nDespite KL's noisier gradient signal, KL-trained predictors produce substantially better masks (Table~\\ref{tab:main}), confirming that \\emph{target alignment} matters more than gradient clarity for mask quality.\nBCE's higher SNR reflects its simpler per-neuron classification objective, which concentrates gradients on individual neuron decisions; KL's coupled gradient distributes signal across all mask decisions jointly, yielding lower SNR but discovering compositionally superior mask patterns (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{snr_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Gradient SNR trajectory across 1000 training steps. BCE (orange) maintains consistently higher SNR than KL (blue) from step 100 onward (2.3--17$\\times$), yet KL produces superior masks (Table~\\ref{tab:main}), suggesting that target alignment matters more than gradient clarity for mask quality.}\n\\label{fig:snr_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\section{Extended Downstream Evaluation}\n\\label{app:downstream}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:downstream} reports HellaSwag and PIQA across sparsity levels and allocation strategies.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Extended downstream benchmarks (LLaMA-3.1-8B). HellaSwag: 10-shot normalized acc; PIQA: 0-shot normalized acc. KL 30\\% uniform: 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; others: seed 42.}\n\\label{tab:downstream}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Method & HellaSwag & PIQA \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% & Dense & \\textbf{82.35} & \\textbf{80.90} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{30\\%}\n& KL uniform & \\textbf{71.07$\\pm$0.54} & \\textbf{74.95$\\pm$0.25} \\\\ % exp_id: hellaswag_piqa_kl30_uniform (3-seed)\n& TEAL uniform & 63.31 & 72.80 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal30_uniform_6task\n& BCE isocompute & 62.81 & 74.21 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_isocompute_30u_hellaswag_piqa\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{50\\%}\n& KL global & \\textbf{58.95} & \\textbf{69.48} \\\\ % exp_id: hellaswag_piqa_kl50_global\n& KL uniform & 56.66 & 69.10 \\\\ % exp_id: hellaswag_piqa_kl50_uniform\n& TEAL uniform & 39.35 & 63.66 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_teal50_uniform_6task\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 30\\%, the KL predictor retains 86.3\\% of dense HellaSwag accuracy (71.07$\\pm$0.54 vs.\\ 82.35, 3-seed) and 92.6\\% of PIQA (74.95$\\pm$0.25 vs.\\ 80.90), confirming moderate degradation on non-reasoning tasks.\nAt 50\\%, global allocation outperforms uniform on HellaSwag (+2.29pp), consistent with the budget-concentration benefit observed for PPL in the capacity-limited regime (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}); PIQA shows a smaller gap (+0.38pp).\n\n\n\\section{Compensation Training Convergence}\n\\label{app:comp_convergence}\n\nAll compensation networks~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} were trained for 1000 steps (lr=$10^{-4}$, batch size 4, seq length 2048). Training loss plateaus by step 700 across all valid runs: 30\\% s42 (final loss 0.70), 30\\% s456 (0.68), 50\\% s42 (1.07), with fluctuations remaining within 0.08 loss units for the final 300 steps. The compensation network converges but provides no quality improvement over the KL predictor alone, confirming that the redundancy result reflects predictor self-sufficiency rather than insufficient compensation training. % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42\n\n\n\\section{Qwen-2.5-14B Detailed Results}\n\\label{app:14b}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Qwen-2.5-14B at 30\\% nominal sparsity. Actual sparsity differs: KL 29.6\\% (3-seed), TEAL 29.3\\% (target 30\\%). \\textbf{Bold}: best sparse per column per group. BCE collapses to 6.4\\% native sparsity (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}), so forced global top-$k$ is used.}\n\\label{tab:14b}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & HS & PIQA \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & 5.19 & 67.58 & 81.06 & 79.77 & 87.57 & 84.20 & 81.94 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\emph{Global top-$k$ allocation}} \\\\\nKL (Ours) & \\textbf{7.62} & 57.17 & 72.06 & 63.72 & 48.82 & \\textbf{75.01} & \\textbf{77.37} \\\\ % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_s42\nTEAL & 8.73 & 56.83 & \\textbf{72.69} & \\textbf{71.83} & 49.51 & 70.51 & 74.70 \\\\ % exp_id: qwen14b_teal30_eval\nBCE & 8.76 & \\textbf{57.59} & 72.61 & 71.67 & \\textbf{53.07} & 53.43 & 68.28 \\\\ % exp_id: qwen14b_bce30_s42_eval\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\emph{Uniform allocation}} \\\\\nKL (Ours) & \\textbf{7.90} & 53.50 & \\textbf{72.82} & 63.55 & 30.9$\\pm$6.8$^*$ & \\textbf{72.68$\\pm$0.2} & \\textbf{76.01$\\pm$1.1} \\\\ % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_uniform (2-seed s42/s456; s123 eval incomplete)\nTEAL & 8.01 & \\textbf{55.29} & 69.85 & \\textbf{66.34} & \\textbf{52.1} & 67.72 & 74.70 \\\\ % exp_id: qwen14b_teal30_uniform\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{8}{l}{\\footnotesize $^*$GSM8K: $n{=}2$ (s42/s456); s123 eval incomplete.} \\\\\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder global allocation, BCE's HellaSwag drops to 53.43\\% ($-$17pp vs.\\ TEAL), indicating that the magnitude-based predictor's mask quality degrades severely when combined with non-uniform budget distribution at 14B scale. % exp_id: qwen14b_bce30_s42_eval, qwen14b_teal30_eval\n\n\\section{Full Allocation Analysis}\n\\label{app:alloc_full}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Allocation summary (LLaMA-3.1-8B). $\\pm$: 3-seed; $^\\ast$high seed variance.}\n\\label{tab:phase}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Allocation & PPL$\\downarrow$ & MMLU \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{30\\%}\n& Global & 8.96$\\pm$0.02 & 35.7$\\pm$9.5$^\\ast$ \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global\n& Uniform & \\textbf{8.81$\\pm$0.02} & \\textbf{40.7$\\pm$1.3} \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{50\\%}\n& Global & \\textbf{11.84$\\pm$0.15} & \\textbf{28.3$\\pm$0.7} \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, kl50_global_s123_reeval, kl50_global_s123_downstream_v2, kl50_global_s456_eval\n& Uniform & 11.92$\\pm$0.07 & 28.0$\\pm$0.6 \\\\ % exp_id: multi_seed_kl50_uniform\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:phase_full} extends Table~\\ref{tab:phase} with all downstream metrics.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Full allocation analysis: global top-$k$ vs.\\ uniform across sparsity levels (LLaMA-3.1-8B). Entries with $\\pm$: 3-seed mean$\\pm$std ($s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$); others: seed 42. $^\\ast$High MMLU seed variance (individual seeds span 25.3--43.5\\%).}\n\\label{tab:phase_full}\n\\begin{tabular}{llccccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Allocation & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{25\\%}\n& Global 8.96$\\pm$0.02 & \\textbf{43.8$\\pm$3.6} & \\textbf{70.2$\\pm$0.9} & 35.7$\\pm$9.5$^\\ast$ & \\textbf{8.0$\\pm$0.7} \\\\\n& Uniform & \\textbf{8.81$\\pm$0.02} & 43.7$\\pm$2.5 & 68.9$\\pm$0.4 & \\textbf{40.7$\\pm$1.3} & 7.5$\\pm$0.1 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{35\\%}\n& Global & \\textbf{9.31} & \\textbf{38.9} & \\textbf{67.6} & \\textbf{40.4} & \\textbf{7.7} \\\\\n& Uniform & 9.44 & 11.92$\\pm$0.07 & 33.8$\\pm$1.2 & 62.9$\\pm$0.1 & 28.0$\\pm$0.6 & 2.2$\\pm$0.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\n\\section{Mask Pattern Visualization}\n\\label{app:mask_viz}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:mask_heatmap} shows per-layer Jaccard distance between KL-derived and magnitude-based masks at 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:scatter} shows per-neuron importance scores at layer 16.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:mask_comparison} shows binary mask comparisons at layers 2, 16, and 28.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{neuron_scatter_layer16.pdf}\n\\caption{KL vs.\\ magnitude importance scores for layer 16 neurons at 50\\% sparsity. 41\\% of neurons fall in disagreement quadrants where one method selects a neuron the other discards.}\n\\label{fig:scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{mask_divergence_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-layer Jaccard distance between KL-derived and magnitude-based masks at 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity (64 samples, 131K tokens). Divergence peaks in layers 24--28 (Jaccard distance $>$0.73 at 50\\%) and increases with sparsity across all layers.}\n\\label{fig:mask_heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{mask_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Binary mask comparison between KL-derived and magnitude-based masks at selected layers (L2, L16, L28) for 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity (64 samples). Black = selected by both, light regions = selected by only one method. Deep layers show the largest divergence (L28 Jaccard distance: 0.60 at 30\\%, 0.74 at 50\\%).}\n\\label{fig:mask_comparison}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\n\\section{Cross-Architecture Comparison}\n\\label{app:cross_arch}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_arch} evaluates on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (GQA + sliding window, dense PPL 5.52).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-architecture comparison (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$). Adv = TEAL\\,/\\,KL ratio. \\textbf{Note:} LLaMA results use global top-$k$ allocation; Mistral results use uniform allocation. Mistral 30\\%: 3-seed mean$\\pm$std; other rows: seed 42.}\n\\label{tab:cross_arch}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{l@{\\hspace{4pt}}cc@{\\hspace{3pt}}c@{\\hspace{6pt}}|@{\\hspace{6pt}}cc@{\\hspace{3pt}}c}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{Mistral-7B} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{LLaMA-8B} \\\\\n & KL & TEAL & Adv & KL & TEAL & Adv \\\\\n\\midrule\n30\\% & \\textbf{6.93$\\pm$0.19} & 8.38 & 1.21$\\times$ & \\textbf{8.96} & 10.97 & 1.22$\\times$ \\\\ % exp_id: mistral_kl30_s42, mistral_kl30_s123, mistral_kl30_s456, teal_30_mistral, kl30_3seed_global\n50\\% & \\textbf{8.65} & 18.31 & 2.12$\\times$ & \\textbf{11.79} & 23.31 & 1.98$\\times$ \\\\ % exp_id: kl_50_mistral, teal_50_mistral\n70\\% & \\textbf{13.33} & 61.72 & 4.63$\\times$ & \\textbf{17.91} & 100.11 & 5.59$\\times$ \\\\ % exp_id: kl_70_mistral, teal_70_mistral\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe KL PPL advantage ratio is consistent across architectures (within 8\\% at $\\leq$50\\% sparsity).\nTable~\\ref{tab:mistral_downstream} reports downstream task accuracy at 30\\% sparsity on Mistral-7B-v0.3 (3-seed). KL improves PPL ($-$17.3\\%) and commonsense tasks (WG +6.7pp) but MMLU is tied (43.3\\% vs.\\ 43.5\\%); ARC-c shows high seed variance ($\\sigma$=6.4pp); GSM8K reverses.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Mistral-7B-v0.3 downstream evaluation at 30\\% sparsity. $\\pm$: 3-seed mean$\\pm$std ($s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$); TEAL is deterministic (single run). ARC-c exhibits high seed variance ($\\sigma$=6.4pp).}\n\\label{tab:mistral_downstream}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & HS & PIQA & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nDense & 5.52 & 60.2 & 78.7 & 83.2 & 81.9 & 62.4 & 36.5 \\\\ % exp_id: lm_eval_mistral_dense, mistral_dense_hellaswag_piqa\nKL 30\\% & \\textbf{6.93$\\pm$0.19} & \\textbf{44.9$\\pm$6.4} & \\textbf{70.2$\\pm$1.2} & \\textbf{69.7$\\pm$0.8} & \\textbf{74.8$\\pm$0.4} & 43.3$\\pm$1.5 & 8.6$\\pm$2.2 \\\\ % exp_id: mistral_kl30_s42, mistral_kl30_s123, mistral_kl30_s456\nTEAL 30\\% & 8.38 & 41.1 & 63.5 & 60.1 & 72.1 & \\textbf{43.5} & \\textbf{15.9} \\\\ % exp_id: mistral_teal30_downstream\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Taxonomy Discussion}\n\\label{app:taxonomy}\n\n\\paragraph{Correction granularity.}\n\\emph{Static} methods~\\cite{xu2025spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.\n\\emph{Per-token} methods~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward,akhauri2024shadowllm,szatkowski2024d2dmoe} produce a unique mask for each input position, conditioning on the token's hidden state at each layer.\n\n\\paragraph{Training signal coupling.}\n\\emph{Decomposed} objectives (BCE) treat each neuron independently, optimizing per-element classification against an oracle mask.\n\\emph{Intermediate} objectives (MSE on hidden states) capture local coupling within a single layer but not end-to-end output effects.\n\\emph{Coupled} objectives (KL on output distribution) propagate gradients through the full forward pass; each mask decision's gradient reflects its joint effect with all other masks on the final distribution.\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2025spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\\times$ coupled).\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nD2DMoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.\nWe define \\emph{output-distribution alignment} as whether the training loss optimizes a quantity that composes across layers.\nKL on output logits directly optimizes next-token prediction loss, which does not suffer from error composition; per-layer MSE minimizes reconstruction error independently at each layer, but layer-wise optima do not compose---a mask minimizing FFN output error at layer $\\ell$ may amplify errors in layers $\\ell{+}1 \\ldots L$.\nThe SNR analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}) corroborates this: KL achieves the best masks despite $2.3\\times$ lower SNR than BCE, while MSE produces the worst masks.\nThe framework predicts that losses operating on output-level quantities should outperform intermediate-level losses, a hypothesis consistent with our MSE results and testable with alternative output-level objectives.\n\n\\section{Oracle Mask Provenance}\n\\label{app:oracle_provenance}\n\nC1's oracle masks are extracted from a converged KL predictor and binarized via hard threshold (logits $> 0$, equivalent to $\\sigma > 0.5$); they reflect one specific training trajectory (optimizer state, data order, Gumbel noise).\nThe s42/s123/s456 consistency ($\\leq$1.70pp) provides partial evidence of robustness, but does not rule out trajectory-dependent confounds.\n\n\\section{SPON Orthogonality}\n\\label{app:spon}\n\nSPON~\\cite{xu2025spon} bias correction is weakly correlated with the KL predictor (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038) and yields only 2.4\\% PPL improvement (12.08$\\to$11.79); the predictor already captures most recoverable signal. % exp_id: spon_decomposition, spon_2048_s42, mvp_kl_norm_v2_s42\nThe decomposition confirms that SPON and the KL predictor operate on largely orthogonal error sources.\n\n\\section{Per-Layer KL Downstream Decomposition}\n\\label{app:perlayer_downstream}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:perlayer_downstream} reports raw downstream numbers for the signal type vs.\\ coupling scope decomposition at 30\\% sparsity (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}). BoolQ's $\\approx$100\\% coupling is an upper bound confounded by high cross-seed variance ($\\pm$11.5pp for per-layer KL); the $K{=}4$ pilot (Table~\\ref{tab:k4_downstream}) suggests much of this gap is train--inference mismatch.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{2D decomposition at 30\\% sparsity (LLaMA-3.1-8B, uniform allocation). All rows are 3-seed mean$\\pm$std. Signal type = per-layer KL $-$ MSE; coupling = E2E KL $-$ per-layer KL. Percentages are upper bounds (dimensions not fully orthogonal; see \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).}\n\\label{tab:perlayer_downstream}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & BoolQ \\\\\n\\midrule\nE2E KL & 8.81$\\pm$0.02 & 43.7 & 68.9 & 40.7 & 7.5 & 73.7 \\\\\nPer-layer KL & 9.05$\\pm$0.02 & 39.9 & 65.9 & 38.6 & 5.8 & 64.5 \\\\\nMSE & 18.52$\\pm$1.68 & 35.8$\\pm$4.1 & 59.8$\\pm$2.6 & 30.7$\\pm$2.6 & 2.3$\\pm$0.7 & 64.5$\\pm$5.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTotal gap & 9.71 & 7.9 & 9.1 & 10.0 & 5.2 & 9.2 \\\\\nSignal type (\\%) & 97.5 & 51.9 & 67.0 & 79.0 & 67.3 & $\\approx$0 \\\\\nCoupling (\\%) & 2.5 & 48.1 & 33.0 & 21.0 & 32.7 & $\\approx$100 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{$K{=}4$ Mismatch Control}\n\\label{app:k4_downstream}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:k4_downstream} reports 3-seed $K{=}4$ per-layer KL results at 30\\% sparsity.\nTraining with $K{=}4$ random layers per step reduces the train--inference mismatch (4/32 vs.\\ 1/32 layers masked during training, while inference applies all 32).\nThe gap closure column reports the fraction of the $K{=}1 \\to$ E2E KL gap closed by $K{=}4$.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{3-seed $K{=}4$ mismatch control at 30\\% sparsity (LLaMA-3.1-8B, uniform allocation, $s \\in \\{42, 123, 456\\}$). Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform). Gap closure $= (K{=}4 - K{=}1) / (\\text{E2E} - K{=}1) \\times 100$.}\n\\label{tab:k4_downstream}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n& $K{=}4$ (3-seed) & $K{=}1$ (3-seed) & E2E KL (3-seed) & Gap closure (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nPPL$\\downarrow$ & 8.89$\\pm$0.01 & 9.05$\\pm$0.02 & 8.81$\\pm$0.02 & 66.7 \\\\\nARC-c & 40.0$\\pm$1.3 & 39.9 & 43.7$\\pm$2.5 & 2.6 \\\\\nWG & 66.9$\\pm$0.2 & 65.9 & 68.9$\\pm$0.4 & 33.3 \\\\\nMMLU$^\\ast$ & 42.7$\\pm$1.4 & 38.6 & 40.7$\\pm$1.3 & $>$100 \\\\\nGSM8K & 7.0$\\pm$1.6 & 5.8 & 7.5$\\pm$0.1 & 70.6 \\\\\nBoolQ$^\\dagger$ & 67.7$\\pm$7.5 & 64.5$\\pm$11.5 & 73.7 & 34.8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $^\\ast$MMLU $K{=}4$ systematically exceeds E2E across all 3 seeds; attributable to $K{=}4$'s near-uniform per-layer sparsity (std=0.003) matching uniform eval allocation. Independently confirmed: removing the predictor's middle-layer valley yields MMLU=44.0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}).\n$^\\dagger$BoolQ exhibits high inter-seed variance for both $K{=}4$ (std=7.5) and $K{=}1$ (std=11.5); gap closure unreliable.}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Sparsity Constraint Analysis}\n\\label{app:constraint}\n\nTo test the causal role of the KL predictor's learned per-layer allocation, we evaluate two inference-time interventions at 30\\% overall sparsity (seed 42, uniform allocation baseline):\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-layer sparsity constraint analysis. \\emph{Protect late}: cap L22--L28 sparsity $\\leq$30\\%; \\emph{Remove valley}: override L14--L18 sparsity to 30\\%. Budget redistributed to maintain 30\\% overall.}\n\\label{tab:constraint}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCondition & PPL & ARC-c & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\nKL baseline (s42) & 8.82 & 40.8 & 40.6 & 7.4 \\\\\nProtect late layers & 9.26 & 42.4 & 40.7 & 6.5 \\\\\nRemove valley & 8.76 & 39.3 & 44.0 & 9.0 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nProtecting late layers (L22--L28) does not improve knowledge-intensive metrics despite reducing their sparsity from 48--57\\% to $\\leq$30\\%, while worsening PPL by 5.2\\%.\nConversely, removing the protection valley (forcing L14--L18 to 30\\% instead of 14--18\\%) improves MMLU by +3.4pp and GSM8K by +1.6pp at the cost of ARC-c $-$1.5pp.\nThe KL predictor's learned allocation thus represents a task-dependent trade-off---prioritizing commonsense reasoning via middle-layer protection---rather than a uniformly optimal pattern.\nThis allocation trade-off also explains the $K{=}4$ MMLU anomaly (\\S\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): $K{=}4$'s near-uniform learned sparsity (std=0.003) inherently removes the valley, producing MMLU=43.5 comparable to the remove-valley condition (44.0).\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Sparsity Distribution}\n\\label{app:perlayer_sparsity}\n\nThe KL predictor at 30\\% target sparsity learns a non-uniform per-layer allocation that differs markedly from magnitude-based selection.\nAggregate sparsity is similar across broad regions (early L0--L7: KL 28.1\\% vs.\\ magnitude 27.5\\%; late L24--L31: 44.9\\% vs.\\ 47.2\\%), but middle layers (L8--L23) exhibit high internal variance despite similar averages (28.2\\% vs.\\ 27.3\\%).\nTable~\\ref{tab:perlayer_sparsity} highlights three sub-ranges.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-layer sparsity sub-ranges: KL predictor vs.\\ magnitude (LLaMA-3.1-8B, 30\\%, uniform allocation). Ranges denote min--max across layers in each group.}\n\\label{tab:perlayer_sparsity}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nLayer Range & KL (\\%) & Mag (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nL8--L12 (high pruning) & 32--36 & 25--26 & $+$7 to $+$10 \\\\\nL14--L18 (protection valley) & 14--18 & 28--30 & $-$8 to $-$16 \\\\\nL22--L28 (aggressive pruning) & 48--57 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{C4 Training Data Control}\n\\label{app:c4_control}\n\nTo test whether WikiText-103's narrow domain drives the knowledge-task regression (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), we train the KL predictor on C4~\\cite{raffel2020c4} (diverse web text) under identical hyperparameters (1000 steps, 30\\% uniform, s42).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{C4-trained vs.\\ WikiText-trained KL predictor (LLaMA-3.1-8B, 30\\% uniform, s42).}\n\\label{tab:c4_control}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nTraining Data & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & HS / PIQA \\\\\n\\midrule\nWikiText-103 & 8.82 & 40.78 & 68.59 & 40.6 & 7.35 & 70.85 / 74.81 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_uniform_hellaswag_piqa\nC4 & 11.55 & 43.09 & 68.67 & 39.95 & 4.09 & 69.24 / 75.03 \\\\ % exp_id: c4_kl30_s42_eval\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe C4-trained predictor achieves comparable MMLU (40.0\\% vs.\\ 40.6\\%) but worse GSM8K (4.1\\% vs.\\ 7.4\\%) and higher PPL (11.55 vs.\\ 8.82), despite training on more diverse text.\nThis rules out training data composition as the primary cause of knowledge-task regression and supports the hypothesis that forward KL's mode-covering property structurally under-protects rare reasoning-critical activations. % exp_id: c4_control_eval\n\n\\section{Per-Layer Coupling Breadth Analysis}\n\\label{app:k_curve}\n\nWe vary the number of layers coupled per training step ($K$) from 1 (pure per-layer) to 32 (end-to-end), evaluating all predictors under TEAL uniform allocation at 30\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{K-curve: coupling breadth vs.\\ predictor quality (30\\%, TEAL uniform, LLaMA-3.1-8B). All values are 3-seed mean$\\pm$std (s42/s123/s456) except $K{=}1$ downstream (single seed). ``---'' = not evaluated.}\n\\label{tab:k_curve}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{cccccccc}\n\\toprule\n$K$ & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K & HS & PIQA \\\\\n\\midrule\n1 & 9.05$\\pm$0.02 & 39.9 & 65.9 & 38.6 & 5.8 & --- & --- \\\\\n2 & 10.75$\\pm$0.15 & 39.7$\\pm$1.2 & 63.2$\\pm$1.7 & 38.3$\\pm$3.2 & 2.8$\\pm$0.6 & 66.5$\\pm$1.0 & 73.5$\\pm$0.7 \\\\\n4 & 8.89$\\pm$0.01 & 40.0$\\pm$1.2 & 66.9$\\pm$0.2 & 42.7$\\pm$1.4 & 7.0$\\pm$1.6 & 67.8$\\pm$1.0 & 74.5$\\pm$0.5 \\\\\n8 & 9.00$\\pm$0.06 & 40.7$\\pm$1.0 & 68.7$\\pm$1.3 & 40.4$\\pm$0.6 & 6.8$\\pm$1.3 & 69.0$\\pm$1.1 & 75.1$\\pm$1.1 \\\\\n16 & 8.67$\\pm$0.02 & 41.9$\\pm$1.1 & 68.5$\\pm$0.9 & 40.9$\\pm$2.9 & 8.5$\\pm$1.2 & 70.1$\\pm$0.4 & 75.0$\\pm$0.8 \\\\\n32 (E2E) & 8.81$\\pm$0.02 & 43.7$\\pm$2.5 & 68.9$\\pm$0.4 & 40.7$\\pm$1.3 & 7.5$\\pm$0.1 & --- & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nPPL exhibits a non-monotonic U-shape (Figure~\\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}): $K{=}2$ is worst (10.75$\\pm$0.15), likely due to insufficient per-layer updates (${\\sim}$62 updates/layer vs.\\ ${\\sim}$250 at $K{=}8$). $K{=}16$ achieves the lowest PPL (8.67$\\pm$0.02), marginally better than E2E (8.81$\\pm$0.02). $K{=}16$ MMLU exhibits high seed variance (std=2.9; s42=44.0, s123=40.0, s456=38.7), with the 3-seed mean (40.9) indistinguishable from E2E (40.7$\\pm$1.3).\nThe signal type gap ($K{=}1$ KL 9.05 vs.\\ per-layer MSE 18.52$\\pm$1.68) of 9.47 PPL dwarfs the coupling breadth range within KL (best $K{=}16$ 8.67 vs.\\ worst $K{=}2$ 10.75 = 2.08 PPL), reinforcing that output-distribution alignment, not coupling scope, is the primary quality lever for PPL at this sparsity level. % exp_id: k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed, perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed\n\n\\section{Licenses}\n\\label{app:licenses}\n\nLLaMA-3.1-8B is released under the Llama 3.1 Community License Agreement.\nMistral-7B-v0.3 is released under the Apache 2.0 License.\nQwen-2.5-14B is released under the Apache 2.0 License.\nWikiText-103 and WikiText-2~\\cite{merity2017pointer} are released under the Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike License (CC BY-SA 3.0).\nAll evaluation benchmarks (ARC, WinoGrande, MMLU, GSM8K, HellaSwag, PIQA) are publicly available under their respective licenses.\nOur code will be released under the MIT License upon acceptance.\n\n\\section{Use of AI Assistants}\n\\label{app:ai-assistants}\n\nWe used Claude (Anthropic) as an AI coding and writing assistant\nthroughout this work. Looking at the results across different tasks, I'm seeing a clear pattern: ARC-c and WG show strong coupling effects that persist even with partial masking, while PPL and GSM8K mostly recover through inference-time matching alone. MMLU is an outlier where the masking strategy actually outperforms end-to-end training, likely because the uniform layer allocation happens to align with how the model is used at inference. At 50% coupling, the partial masking approach performs worse than full masking but still far better than end-to-end, suggesting there's a threshold effect where some minimum gradient isolation is needed.

The K-curve analysis reveals that the coupling effects aren't monotonic—increasing the number of masked layers helps up to a point (K=16 gives the best perplexity), but the overall range of variation from coupling is actually quite small compared to the fundamental difference between signal types, indicating that while layer-wise masking matters, it's a secondary factor compared to what's being masked. I'm breaking down the edits I need to make to the experiments.tex file. First, I'll replace the Per-Layer KL paragraph and its body with a more concise version, then remove the standalone K-curve sentence that follows. I'll need to be careful about matching the exact text strings so the edits apply correctly. The results break down into three categories: coupling-dominated tasks like ARC-c and WordsGame show substantial cross-layer benefits, while mismatch-dominated tasks like perplexity and GSM8K mostly recover through the control, and MMLU anomalously exceeds the end-to-end baseline due to the uniform allocation matching inference. At 50% sparsity, partial coupling actually performs worse than full decoupling, suggesting gradient interference becomes problematic under tighter constraints. The K-curve analysis shows that per-layer KL with full coupling ($K=16$) nearly matches end-to-end performance, but the choice of signal type—optimizing the output distribution versus intermediate activations—creates a much larger gap than varying the coupling scope, with KL achieving 9.05 PPL at $K=1$ while MSE degrades to 18.52.

However, this comparison is complicated by training-inference mismatch, unequal per-layer update counts, and an anomalous failure case where $K=1$ actually outperforms $K=4$ on one metric while end-to-end remains superior. A controlled $K=4$ experiment with near-uniform sparsity helps isolate these confounds and shows that the PPL and downstream task gaps are largely attributable to the signal type choice rather than coupling breadth. I'm restructuring the contribution list to highlight three key empirical findings: first, the per-layer KL decomposition showing that signal-type gaps dominate coupling effects, second, cross-scale validation that output-distribution losses consistently outperform intermediate approaches, and third, the task-dependent split revealing directional gains in commonsense reasoning but knowledge regression across model scales.

Then I'm adding two analytical contributions: the target-swap ablation demonstrating that KL-discovered masks transfer to BCE with improved downstream fidelity, and the finding that these patterns eliminate the need for post-hoc compensation across sparsity levels.

The key distinction is that existing training-free methods rely on fixed statistics or input-agnostic criteria, whereas my approach learns input-dependent masks optimized to preserve output fidelity.

  "new_string": "On LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with directional commonsense gains ($n{=}3$) but knowledge-intensive regression (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp, CIs overlap; 14B GSM8K $-$21.2pp, $n{=}2$); post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (PPL gap $\\leq$0.65pp; 3-seed, 30\\%).\nPer-layer controls show the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL) at 30\\%, though the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch; a $K{=}4$ control suggests task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer})."

_source line 11190 · 2026-05-24T03:35:57.039Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HNFkaDJpHPzYQRnppKB2fi`</summary>

```text
1	Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.
2	We train lightweight predictors ($<$1\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.
3	On LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with directional commonsense gains ($n{=}3$) but knowledge-intensive regression (MMLU $-$5.2pp on 8B; GSM8K $-$19.9pp on 14B); post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.
4	Output-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (PPL gap $\leq$0.65pp; 3-seed, 30\%).
5	Signal type (output-distribution vs.\ intermediate loss) may account for a substantial portion of the PPL gap at moderate sparsity (30\%) (upper bound; \S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
6	On downstream tasks, cross-layer coupling contributes substantially for commonsense reasoning but minimally for mathematical reasoning (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected decomposition).
7	Cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.
8	This is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.
9	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	How the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.
4	Learned sparsity predictors~\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.
5	Yet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.
6	This mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.
7	Unlike MaskLLM~\cite{fang2024maskllm}, which applies KL to static N:M weight masks, we address contextual activation sparsity where masks must generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.
8	
9	Gradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{BCE}} / \partial s_j = \sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}).
10	KL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\% from magnitude-based masks (mean per-layer Jaccard distance).
11	A target-swap ablation shows KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information: BCE retrained on KL-derived targets approaches KL PPL ($\leq$0.65pp gap, 3-seed), while magnitude-trained BCE yields 28.13$\pm$0.20. Coupling contributes marginally to PPL but meaningfully to downstream fidelity (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
12	
13	We instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.
14	At 30\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\ 10.30, both at 28.5\%) with downstream (MMLU 40.7$\pm$1.3\% vs.\ 65.3\% dense, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\times$).
15	At 50\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.
16	
17	Cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\%) with directional commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$19.9pp, $\sigma$=5.3pp; MMLU $-$2.8pp).
18	This paper is primarily an empirical study characterizing how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality across allocation strategies, sparsity levels, and model scales. The central finding---that output-distribution alignment consistently and substantially outperforms per-neuron classification for learned sparsity---is the paper's contribution. Without sparse kernel support, the predictor adds 6.4\% latency overhead; wall-clock speedup is not addressed (Appendix~\ref{app:latency}).
19	While individual components (KL divergence, Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation) are established techniques, their application to contextual activation sparsity prediction has not been explored, and the resulting empirical behaviors are non-obvious.
20	
21	Our contributions are:
22	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
23	\item \emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with exploratory per-layer KL decomposition into signal type (may account for most of the PPL gap; upper bound) and coupling scope (task-dependent; $K{=}4$-corrected; \S\ref{sec:perlayer});
24	\item \emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
25	\item \emph{Practical:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond PPL (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
26	\item \emph{Practical:} These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\S\ref{sec:compensation}).
27	\end{itemize}
28	
29	\begin{figure}[t]
30	\centering
31	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}
32	\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}
33	\label{fig:overview}
34	\end{figure}
35	
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	
6	\paragraph{Model and Predictor.}
7	We evaluate on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}, which uses SwiGLU FFN blocks~\cite{shazeer2020glu} with $d_{\text{model}}=4096$ and $d_{\text{ff}}=14336$ across 32 layers.
8	The sparsity predictor is a two-layer MLP with 2.37M parameters per layer (75.9M total), implemented as Linear($4096 \to 128$) + GELU + Linear($128 \to 14336$), trained to produce per-neuron importance scores that minimize output-distribution divergence under sparsity.
9	
10	\paragraph{Training.}
11	All predictors are trained for 1000 steps on WikiText-103 with sequence length 2048 and batch size 4 on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\,GB), using AdamW~\cite{loshchilov2019adamw} with learning rate $1 \times 10^{-3}$, weight decay $1 \times 10^{-2}$, and cosine decay.
12	The KL predictor uses Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{jang2017gumbel} with temperature $\tau=1.0$ annealed to $0.1$ over training.
13	
14	\paragraph{Evaluation.}
15	We report perplexity on WikiText-2~\cite{merity2017pointer} (seq\_len=2048) and downstream accuracy on ARC-Challenge (25-shot), WinoGrande (5-shot), MMLU (5-shot), GSM8K (8-shot), HellaSwag (10-shot), and PIQA (0-shot).
16	
17	\paragraph{Inference Protocol.}
18	At inference, the predictor produces per-neuron scores that are passed to TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\cite{liu2025teal}, which distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly.
19	We term this \textit{predictor-TEAL}: the predictor replaces TEAL's magnitude-based scoring while retaining its allocation strategy.
20	
21	\paragraph{Baselines.}
22	We compare against: (1) \textbf{Vanilla TEAL}~\cite{liu2025teal}, which uses activation magnitudes directly\footnote{TEAL is deterministic; single-run evaluation suffices. KL/BCE predictors require multi-seed evaluation due to random initialization.}; (2) \textbf{BCE predictor} (DejaVu-style~\cite{liu2023dejavu}), trained with per-neuron binary cross-entropy on magnitude-based oracle masks for 1000 and 2700 steps; and (3) \textbf{Uniform allocation}, which distributes the sparsity budget equally across layers.
23	
24	
25	\subsection{Main Results}
26	
27	Table~\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison. Uniform allocation preserves downstream performance best; global allocation is analyzed to understand allocation-quality tradeoffs (\S\ref{sec:scaling}).
28	At 30\% sparsity, output-distribution KL under uniform allocation achieves PPL 8.81$\pm$0.02 (3-seed), a 14.5\% reduction over TEAL at matched actual sparsity (8.81 vs.\ 10.30, both at 28.5\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ood}).
29	Uniform allocation yields stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\pm$1.3\%, 3-seed; Appendix~\ref{app:stability}), while global top-$k$ exhibits high seed variance (35.7$\pm$9.5\%) from late-layer starvation (L31 retention 28.4\%; \S\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_s123_uniform, kl30_s456_uniform, kl30_3seed
30	
31	\paragraph{Task-Type Split.}
32	KL shows directional improvements over TEAL on commonsense reasoning (ARC-c, WG, HellaSwag, PIQA: 4/4 wins at 30\% uniform) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive tasks (MMLU, GSM8K: 0/2).
33	With only three seeds, 95\% confidence intervals for individual downstream tasks overlap; we therefore treat PPL ($\sigma{=}0.02$, non-overlapping) as the primary metric and downstream differences as directional evidence.
34	The KL predictor protects L14--L18 (14--18\% sparsity vs.\ 32\% global mean) while aggressively pruning L22--L28 (48--57\%). Constraint interventions (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}) confirm a task-dependent trade-off: removing the valley improves MMLU +3.4pp but reduces ARC-c by 1.5pp, consistent with localized factual associations~\cite{geva2023dissecting}; task-aware allocation may resolve this. % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, teal30_uniform, hellaswag_piqa_kl30_uniform, perlayer_sparsity_analysis, constraint_protect_late, constraint_remove_valley
35	
36	\paragraph{Knowledge-Task Regression: Hypothesis.}
37	We consider two hypotheses. First, WikiText-103's limited mathematical content may bias allocation against reasoning pathways---however, a C4-trained control does not support this (see below). Second, forward KL's mode-covering property distributes mass across high-frequency patterns, potentially diluting rare activations encoding factual knowledge concentrated in specific FFN neurons~\cite{meng2022locating}. Constraint experiments (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}) confirm allocation contributes: removing the learned valley improves MMLU +3.4pp and GSM8K +1.6pp. A predictor trained on C4 (diverse web text; Appendix~\ref{app:c4_control}) does not alleviate the regression (MMLU 40.0\%, GSM8K 4.1\% vs.\ 40.7\%, 7.5\%), confirming the deficit is structural to output-distribution KL rather than training data composition. % exp_id: constraint_remove_valley, c4_control_eval
38	
39	At 50\%, the PPL advantage widens (11.92 vs.\ 23.90, 50\% reduction) and generalizes to C4 (Appendix~\ref{app:ood}), but all methods collapse to near-random on MMLU and GSM8K ($\leq$2.7\%); no allocation strategy recovers at this capacity limit. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, kl50_global_s123_downstream_v2, kl50_global_s456_eval, d030_kl50_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global
40	
41	\begin{table}[t]
42	\centering
43	\caption{Main results on LLaMA-3.1-8B. $^\ast$$2.71\times$ KL compute (\S\ref{sec:isocompute}). $\pm$: 3-seed; unmarked: s42. $^\ddagger$Actual sparsity: KL 28.5\% vs.\ TEAL 29.3\% at 30\% (Appendix~\ref{app:ood}).}
44	\label{tab:main}
45	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{
46	\begin{tabular}{llccccc}
47	\toprule
48	Sparsity & Method & PPL$\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\
49	\midrule
50	0\% & Dense & 6.12 & 58.28 & 78.06 & 65.27 & 48.75 \\
51	\midrule
52	\multirow{6}{*}{30\%}
53	& TEAL uniform & 10.82 & 39.33 & 65.04 & \textbf{45.86} & \textbf{8.79} \\ % exp_id: teal30_uniform
54	& BCE global & 11.25 & 41.2 & 66.0 & 36.3 & 6.0 \\ % exp_id: bce30_teal_global
55	& BCE uniform & 15.09$\pm$0.13 & 33.45 & 61.09 & 43.05 & 3.97 \\ % exp_id: bce_iso_v3_s42, bce_iso_v3_s123, bce_iso_v3_s456
56	& MSE uniform & 18.52$\pm$1.68 & 35.8$\pm$4.1 & 59.8$\pm$2.6 & 30.7$\pm$2.6 & 2.3$\pm$0.7 \\ % exp_id: mse30_3seed
57	& KL global (Ours)$^\ddagger$ & 8.96$\pm$0.02 & \textbf{43.8$\pm$3.6} & \textbf{70.2$\pm$0.9} & 35.7$\pm$9.5 & 8.0$\pm$0.7 \\ % exp_id: kl30_3seed_global
58	& KL uniform$^\ddagger$ & \textbf{8.81$\pm$0.02} & 43.7$\pm$2.5 & 68.9$\pm$0.4 & 40.7$\pm$1.3 & 7.5$\pm$0.1 \\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_s123_uniform, kl30_s456_uniform
59	\midrule
60	\multirow{5}{*}{50\%}

71	\subsection{Extended Training Analysis}
72	\label{sec:isocompute}
73	
74	At $2.71\times$ KL compute budget (2700 steps, matched wall-clock), BCE achieves PPL 28.13$\pm$0.20 (3-seed), still $2.39\times$ worse than KL (11.79). % exp_id: bce50_3seed
75	The gap is systematic: BCE reaches higher oracle F1 (0.613 vs.\ 0.540) with 139\% worse PPL, confirming KL optimizes for distributional fidelity rather than magnitude agreement. % exp_id: f1_oracle_analysis, bce_isocompute_s42
76	
77	
78	\subsection{Mask Pattern Analysis}
79	\label{sec:mask_analysis}
80	
81	KL-derived masks diverge from magnitude oracles: per-layer Jaccard distance averages 68\% at 50\% (64 samples, 131K tokens; Figure~\ref{fig:mask_heatmap}), with cross-seed variation (36.3\%) confirming systematic divergence (Appendices~\ref{app:cross_seed_jaccard},~\ref{app:mask_viz}).
82	Despite this, KL achieves PPL 11.79 vs.\ BCE's 28.13$\pm$0.20, confirming that distributional fidelity, not magnitude agreement, produces better masks. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, bce50_3seed, c1_mask_overlap
83	Divergence follows a U-shaped per-layer pattern, peaking in late layers (0.70--0.75 at 50\%). % exp_id: c1_mask_overlap
84	
85	\paragraph{Target-Swap Ablation: Target Quality vs.\ Gradient Coupling.}
86	To disentangle whether KL's advantage stems from its coupled gradient pathway or from the mask patterns it discovers, we train a standard BCE predictor on KL-derived oracle masks instead of magnitude-based masks (C1 target-swap ablation).
87	Table~\ref{tab:c1} reports results under TEAL uniform allocation at matched sparsity.
88	
89	\begin{table}[t]
90	\centering
91	\caption{Target-swap ablation (C1): BCE on KL-derived masks vs.\ KL predictor (same seed), TEAL uniform. All seeds show max $\Delta$$\leq$1.70pp at 30\%; max $\Delta$$\leq$0.79pp at 50\% (s42). $^\dagger$\textbf{Provenance:} s42 oracle extracted from an earlier training run (not seed-matched); s123/s456 use seed-matched retrained oracles (Appendix~\ref{app:oracle_provenance}).}
92	\label{tab:c1}
93	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{

100	& & KL & \textbf{8.82} & \textbf{40.78} & \textbf{68.59} & 40.6 & 7.35 \\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform
101	& s123 & C1 & 8.84 & 44.71 & \textbf{69.77} & \textbf{39.92} & 6.82 \\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s123

112	
113	At 50\% (s42, earlier oracle), C1 matches KL within 0.79pp, while magnitude-trained BCE yields $2.4\times$ worse PPL (Table~\ref{tab:main}).
114	At 30\%, retrained seeds show $\leq$1.18pp agreement\footnote{s42 oracle from earlier checkpoint (gap 0.65pp); s123/s456 use seed-matched converged oracles ($\leq$0.03pp gap).}.
115	C1 demonstrates that KL-discovered mask patterns \emph{transfer} to a simpler training mechanism (PPL within 0.79pp; downstream variation up to 4.5pp MMLU); per-layer KL controls (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}) further show coupling contributes marginally to PPL but substantially to downstream fidelity.
116	However, C1 also changes mask representation: binarizing oracle soft logits ($>$0) into hard masks discards ranking information, so the near-zero delta reflects transferability of binary mask \emph{patterns} rather than full predictor knowledge. BCE+Gumbel (32.22) vs.\ standard BCE (27.97) suggests softness alone does not explain KL's advantage, though trajectory-dependent confounds remain (Appendix~\ref{app:oracle_provenance}). % exp_id: bce50_3seed, c1_bce_kl_targets_30u, c1_s123, bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, eval_bce_v3_s42
117	
118	\subsection{Ablation Studies}
119

150	
151	\paragraph{Relaxation, Divergence, and SPON.}
152	At 50\%, Gumbel-Sigmoid outperforms STE~\cite{bengio2013ste} by 33.8\% (11.79 vs.\ 15.77); forward KL outperforms JSD (+6.4\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\%), consistent with mode-covering~\cite{gu2024minillm}. % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42, jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42
153	At 30\%, forward KL (8.81) and JSD (8.90) are near-identical, both outperforming reverse KL (9.26) and MSE (18.52$\pm$1.68). % exp_id: kl30_ablation_s42, jsd30_ablation_s42, rkl30_ablation_s42, mse30_uniform_s42, mse30_3seed
154	KL--JSD near-equivalence suggests the divergence measure matters less than optimizing through the output distribution; coupling scope provides additional downstream benefits (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}). % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, rkl30_downstream_3seed
155	\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Exploratory Decomposition of Signal Type and Coupling Scope.}
156	\label{sec:perlayer}
157	To isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.
158	At $K{=}1$, per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\pm$0.02, within 2.7\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\pm$1.68 ($2.05\times$ worse). For PPL, signal type\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} may account for a substantial portion of the gap---an upper bound, as $K{=}1$ conflates coupling removal with train--inference mismatch. A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} partially deconfounds this (Appendix~\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c (97\% residual gap) and WG (67\%) indicate genuine cross-layer benefits; \emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\% closure) and GSM8K (71\%) largely close; \emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E (42.7$\pm$1.4 vs.\ 40.7$\pm$1.3), explained by $K{=}4$'s near-uniform allocation matching uniform inference (Appendix~\ref{app:constraint}). % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed
159	At 50\%, partial coupling ($K{=}4$: 44.23) is \emph{worse} than $K{=}1$ (34.85) while E2E achieves 11.84, suggesting gradient interference under tight capacity; the decomposition is interpretable only at 30\%. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42, perlayer_kl_50_s42, bench_kl50_global
160	
161	\begin{figure}[t]
162	\centering
163	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{k_curve_ppl}
164	\vspace{-8pt}
165	\caption{Coupling breadth $K$ vs.\ perplexity (30\%, TEAL uniform, 3-seed mean$\pm$std). $K{=}32$ denotes end-to-end training. $K{=}2$ degradation reflects insufficient per-layer updates (${\sim}62$ vs.\ ${\sim}250$ at $K{=}8$).}
166	\label{fig:k_curve_ppl}
167	\vspace{-8pt}
168	\end{figure}
169	
170	The $K$-curve (Figure~\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}) confirms non-monotonic coupling effects: $K{=}16$ achieves the lowest PPL (8.67$\pm$0.02 vs.\ E2E 8.81$\pm$0.02), yet the full coupling range (2.08 PPL) is dwarfed by the signal type gap (9.47 PPL). % exp_id: k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed
171	
172	\paragraph{Compensation Redundancy.}
173	\label{sec:compensation}
174	FastForward-style compensation~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} (+67.2M params) provides no benefit (Appendices~\ref{app:comp_convergence}--\ref{app:training_convergence}). % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42, bce_comp_staged_s42, db_sensitivity_sweep, tau_schedule_sweep, training_steps_sweep

178	\label{sec:scaling}

188	
189	\paragraph{Allocation Behavior.}
190	Table~\ref{tab:phase} (Appendix~\ref{app:alloc_full}) reveals non-monotonic allocation preference: uniform is best at $\leq$30\% ($7.3\times$ smaller MMLU $\sigma$), global at 35--40\%, both collapse at $\geq$45\%. % exp_id: kl30_3seed, regime_kl35_global, regime_kl35_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global, multi_seed_kl50_uniform
191	
192	\paragraph{Cross-Architecture and Scale.}
193	\label{sec:cross_arch}
194	On Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral}, PPL generalizes ($-$17.3\%, 3-seed; Table~\ref{tab:mistral_downstream}); on Qwen-2.5-14B~\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, PPL generalizes but MMLU/GSM8K decline (Table~\ref{tab:14b}).
195	The consistent regression across scales (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp, 14B GSM8K 32.2$\pm$5.3 vs.\ 52.1) suggests a systematic property of output-distribution KL; Qwen's stronger reasoning baseline (GSM8K 87.6\% vs.\ 48.8\% dense) may amplify sensitivity to distribution-based pruning. The pipeline adds 6.4\% latency overhead absent sparse kernels (Appendix~\ref{app:latency}). % exp_id: mistral_kl30_s42, mistral_kl30_s123, mistral_kl30_s456, mistral_teal30_downstream, qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform, latency_profiling

      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Model and Predictor.}\nWe evaluate on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}, which uses SwiGLU FFN blocks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} with $d_{\\text{model}}=4096$ and $d_{\\text{ff}}=14336$ across 32 layers.\nThe sparsity predictor is a two-layer MLP with 2.37M parameters per layer (75.9M total), implemented as Linear($4096 \\to 128$) + GELU + Linear($128 \\to 14336$), trained to produce per-neuron importance scores that minimize output-distribution divergence under sparsity.\n\n\\paragraph{Training.}\nAll predictors are trained for 1000 steps on WikiText-103 with sequence length 2048 and batch size 4 on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (96\\,GB), using AdamW~\\cite{loshchilov2019adamw} with learning rate $1 \\times 10^{-3}$, weight decay $1 \\times 10^{-2}$, and cosine decay.\nThe KL predictor uses Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} with temperature $\\tau=1.0$ annealed to $0.1$ over training.\n\n\\paragraph{Evaluation.}\nWe report perplexity on WikiText-2~\\cite{merity2017pointer} (seq\\_len=2048) and downstream accuracy on ARC-Challenge (25-shot), WinoGrande (5-shot), MMLU (5-shot), GSM8K (8-shot), HellaSwag (10-shot), and PIQA (0-shot).\n\n\\paragraph{Inference Protocol.}\nAt inference, the predictor produces per-neuron scores that are passed to TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation mechanism~\\cite{liu2025teal}, which distributes the sparsity budget across layers non-uniformly.\nWe term this \\textit{predictor-TEAL}: the predictor replaces TEAL's magnitude-based scoring while retaining its allocation strategy.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare against: (1) \\textbf{Vanilla TEAL}~\\cite{liu2025teal}, which uses activation magnitudes directly\\footnote{TEAL is deterministic; single-run evaluation suffices. KL/BCE predictors require multi-seed evaluation due to random initialization.}; (2) \\textbf{BCE predictor} (DejaVu-style~\\cite{liu2023dejavu}), trained with per-neuron binary cross-entropy on magnitude-based oracle masks for 1000 and 2700 steps; and (3) \\textbf{Uniform allocation}, which distributes the sparsity budget equally across layers.\n\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main} presents the primary comparison. Uniform allocation preserves downstream performance best; global allocation is analyzed to understand allocation-quality tradeoffs (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\nAt 30\\% sparsity, output-distribution KL under uniform allocation achieves PPL 8.81$\\pm$0.02 (3-seed), a 14.5\\% reduction over TEAL at matched actual sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30, both at 28.5\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nUniform allocation yields stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed; Appendix~\\ref{app:stability}), while global top-$k$ exhibits high seed variance (35.7$\\pm$9.5\\%) from late-layer starvation (L31 retention 28.4\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:scaling}). % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, kl30_s123_uniform, kl30_s456_uniform, kl30_3seed\n\n\\paragraph{Task-Type Split.}\nKL shows directional improvements over TEAL on commonsense reasoning (ARC-c, WG, HellaSwag, PIQA: 4/4 wins at 30\\% uniform) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive tasks (MMLU, GSM8K: 0/2).\nWith only three seeds, 95\\% confidence intervals for individual downstream tasks overlap; we therefore treat PPL ($\\sigma{=}0.02$, non-overlapping) as the primary metric and downstream differences as directional evidence.\nThe KL predictor protects L14--L18 (14--18\\% sparsity vs.\\ 32\\% global mean) while aggressively pruning L22--L28 (48--57\\%). Constraint interventions (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}) confirm a task-dependent trade-off: removing the valley improves MMLU +3.4pp but reduces ARC-c by 1.5pp, consistent with localized factual associations~\\cite{geva2023dissecting}; task-aware allocation may resolve this. % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform, teal30_uniform, hellaswag_piqa_kl30_uniform, perlayer_sparsity_analysis, constraint_protect_late, constraint_remove_valley\n\n\\paragraph{Knowledge-Task Regression: Hypothesis.}\nWe consider two hypotheses. First, WikiText-103's limited mathematical content may bias allocation against reasoning pathways---however, a C4-trained control does not support this (see below). Second, forward KL's mode-covering property distributes mass across high-frequency patterns, potentially diluting rare activations encoding factual knowledge concentrated in specific FFN neurons~\\cite{meng2022locating}. Constraint experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}) confirm allocation contributes: removing the learned valley improves MMLU +3.4pp and GSM8K +1.6pp. A predictor trained on C4 (diverse web text; Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_control}) does not alleviate the regression (MMLU 40.0\\%, GSM8K 4.1\\% vs.\\ 40.7\\%, 7.5\\%), confirming the deficit is structural to output-distribution KL rather than training data composition. % exp_id: constraint_remove_valley, c4_control_eval\n\nAt 50\\%, the PPL advantage widens (11.92 vs.\\ 23.90, 50\\% reduction) and generalizes to C4
