Analysis}\n\\label{sec:mask_analysis}\n\nKL-derived masks diverge from magnitude oracles: per-layer Jaccard distance averages 68\\% at 50\\% (64 samples, 131K tokens; Figure~\\ref{fig:mask_heatmap}), with cross-seed variation (36.3\\%) confirming systematic divergence (Appendices~\\ref{app:cross_seed_jaccard},~\\ref{app:mask_viz}).\nDespite this, KL achieves PPL 11.79 vs.\\ BCE's 28.13$\\pm$0.20, confirming that distributional fidelity, not magnitude agreement, produces better masks. % exp_id: bench_kl50_global, bce50_3seed, c1_mask_overlap\nDivergence follows a U-shaped per-layer pattern, peaking in late layers (0.70--0.75 at 50\\%). % exp_id: c1_mask_overlap\n\n\\paragraph{Target-Swap Ablation: Target Quality vs.\\ Gradient Coupling.}\nTo disentangle whether KL's advantage stems from its coupled gradient pathway or from the mask patterns it discovers, we train a standard BCE predictor on KL-derived oracle masks instead of magnitude-based masks (C1 target-swap ablation).\nTable~\\ref{tab:c1} reports results under TEAL uniform allocation at matched sparsity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Target-swap ablation (C1): BCE on KL-derived masks vs.\\ KL predictor (same seed), TEAL uniform. All seeds show max $\\Delta$$\\leq$1.70pp at 30\\%; max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\% (s42). $^\\dagger$\\textbf{Provenance:} s42 oracle extracted from an earlier training run (not seed-matched); s123/s456 use seed-matched retrained oracles (Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle_provenance}).}\n\\label{tab:c1}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccccc}\n\\toprule\nSparsity & Seed & Method & PPL$\\downarrow$ & ARC-c & WG & MMLU & GSM8K \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{6}{*}{30\\%}\n& s42$^\\dagger$ & C1 & 9.45 & 40.27 & 67.40 & \\textbf{42.30} & \\textbf{7.58} \\\\ % exp_id: c1_ablation_30pct_s42_eval_v2\n& & KL & \\textbf{8.82} & \\textbf{40.78} & \\textbf{68.59} & 40.6 & 7.35 \\\\ % exp_id: bench_kl30_uniform\n& s123 & C1 & 8.84 & 44.71 & \\textbf{69.77} & \\textbf{39.92} & 6.82 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s123\n& & KL & \\textbf{8.83} & \\textbf{45.31} & 69.38 & 39.4 & \\textbf{7.51} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_s123_eval\n& s456 & C1 & 8.82 & 44.71 & \\textbf{69.93} & 41.04 & 6.60 \\\\ % exp_id: eval_c1_retrain_30pct_s456\n& & KL & \\textbf{8.79} & \\textbf{45.05} & 68.75 & \\textbf{42.0} & \\textbf{7.58} \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_s456_uniform_eval\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{2}{*}{50\\%}\n& s42 & C1 & 11.96 & 32.17 & \\textbf{63.14} & 27.79 & \\textbf{2.05} \\\\ % exp_id: c1_bce_kl_targets_teal50u\n& & KL & 11.96 & \\textbf{32.59} & 62.35 & \\textbf{27.92} & 1.97 \\\\ % exp_id: c1_kl_teal50u\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt 50\\% (s42, earlier oracle), C1 matches KL within 0.79pp, while magnitude-trained BCE yields $2.4\\times$ worse PPL (Table~\\ref{tab:main}).\nAt 30\\%, retrained seeds show $\\leq$1.18pp agreement\\footnote{s42 oracle from earlier checkpoint (gap 0.65pp); s123/s456 use seed-matched converged oracles ($\\leq$0.03pp gap).}.\nC1 demonstrates that KL-discovered mask patterns \\emph{transfer} to a simpler training mechanism (PPL within 0.79pp; downstream variation up to 4.5pp MMLU); per-layer KL controls (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}) further show coupling contributes marginally to PPL but substantially to downstream fidelity.\nHowever, C1 also changes mask representation: binarizing oracle soft logits ($>$0) into hard masks discards ranking information, so the near-zero delta reflects transferability of binary mask \\emph{patterns} rather than full predictor knowledge. BCE+Gumbel (32.22) vs.\\ standard BCE (27.97) suggests softness alone does not explain KL's advantage, though trajectory-dependent confounds remain (Appendix~\\ref{app:oracle_provenance}). % exp_id: bce50_3seed, c1_bce_kl_targets_30u, c1_s123, bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, eval_bce_v3_s42\n\n\\subsection{Ablation Studies}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:ablation} isolates the contribution of each design choice at 30\\% and 50\\% sparsity.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Ablation on LLaMA-3.1-8B (WikiText-2 PPL$\\downarrow$; uniform at 30\\%, global at 50\\%). $\\pm$: 3-seed; unmarked: s42. $^\\ddagger$STE: 20.8\\% actual sparsity at 30\\% target; loss effect ($\\Delta$PPL=20.4) is $5.1\\times$ relaxation effect ($\\Delta$PPL=3.98). $^\\S$MSE 50\\%: s42 only; s123 catastrophic (PPL{=}15{,}789; Appendix~\\ref{app:mse_config}).}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nConfiguration & 30\\% PPL$\\downarrow$ & 50\\% PPL$\\downarrow$ & $\\Delta_{50}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{KL-based variants}} \\\\\nForward KL + Gumbel (Ours) & \\textbf{8.81$\\pm$0.02} & \\textbf{11.79} & --- \\\\ % exp_id: kl30_ablation_s42, bench_kl50_global\nJSD (symmetric) + Gumbel & 8.90$\\pm$0.02 & 12.54 & +0.75 \\\\ % exp_id: jsd30_ablation_s42, jsd_ablation_s42\nReverse KL + Gumbel & 9.26$\\pm$0.07 & 12.80 & +1.01 \\\\ % exp_id: rkl30_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nForward KL + STE & ---$^\\ddagger$ & 15.77 & +3.98 \\\\ % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Per-layer training}} \\\\\nPer-layer MSE & 18.52$\\pm$1.68 & 229.26$^\\S$ & +217.47 \\\\ % exp_id: mse30_uniform_s42, mse50_uniform_s42\nPer-layer KL ($K{=}1$) & 9.05$\\pm$0.02 & 34.85 & +23.06 \\\\ % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_50_s42\nPer-layer KL ($K{=}4$) & 8.89$\\pm$0.01 & 44.23 & +32.44 \\\\ % exp_id: perlayer_kl_k4_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{BCE-based variants}} \\\\\nBCE (no compensation) & 15.71 & 27.97 & +16.18 \\\\ % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, eval_bce_v3_s42\nBCE + Gumbel (no penalty) & --- & 32.22 & +20.43 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42\nBCE + Gumbel + Penalty & --- & 38.02 & +26.23 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation\nBCE + STE & --- & 60.54 & +48.75 \\\\ % exp_id: bce_ste_s42\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Relaxation, Divergence, and SPON.}\nAt 50\\%, Gumbel-Sigmoid outperforms STE~\\cite{bengio2013ste} by 33.8\\% (11.79 vs.\\ 15.77); forward KL outperforms JSD (+6.4\\%) and reverse KL (+8.6\\%), consistent with mode-covering~\\cite{gu2024minillm}. % exp_id: ste_baseline_s42, jsd_ablation_s42, reverse_kl_ablation_s42\nAt 30\\%, forward KL (8.81) and JSD (8.90) are near-identical, both outperforming reverse KL (9.26) and MSE (18.52$\\pm$1.68). % exp_id: kl30_ablation_s42, jsd30_ablation_s42, rkl30_ablation_s42, mse30_uniform_s42, mse30_3seed\nKL--JSD near-equivalence suggests the divergence measure matters less than optimizing through the output distribution; coupling scope provides additional downstream benefits (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}). % exp_id: bce30_uniform_s42, rkl30_downstream_3seed\n\\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Exploratory Decomposition of Signal Type and Coupling Scope.}\n\\label{sec:perlayer}\nTo isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.\nAt $K{=}1$, per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse). For PPL, signal type\\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} may account for a substantial portion of the gap---an upper bound, as $K{=}1$ conflates coupling removal with train--inference mismatch. A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} partially deconfounds this (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \\emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c (97\\% residual gap) and WG (67\\%) indicate genuine cross-layer benefits; \\emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\\% closure) and GSM8K (71\\%) largely close; \\emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E (42.7$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3), explained by $K{=}4$'s near-uniform allocation matching uniform inference (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}). % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed\nAt 50\\%, partial coupling ($K{=}4$: 44.23) is \\emph{worse} than $K{=}1$ (34.85) while E2E achieves 11.84, suggesting gradient interference under tight capacity; the decomposition is interpretable only at 30\\%. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42, perlayer_kl_50_s42, bench_kl50_global\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{k_curve_ppl}\n\\vspace{-8pt}\n\\caption{Coupling breadth $K$ vs.\\ perplexity (30\\%, TEAL uniform, 3-seed mean$\\pm$std). $K{=}32$ denotes end-to-end training. $K{=}2$ degradation reflects insufficient per-layer updates (${\\sim}62$ vs.\\ ${\\sim}250$ at $K{=}8$).}\n\\label{fig:k_curve_ppl}\n\\vspace{-8pt}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe $K$-curve (Figure~\\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}) confirms non-monotonic coupling effects: $K{=}16$ achieves the lowest PPL (8.67$\\pm$0.02 vs.\\ E2E 8.81$\\pm$0.02), yet the full coupling range (2.08 PPL) is dwarfed by the signal type gap (9.47 PPL). % exp_id: k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed\n\n\\paragraph{Compensation Redundancy.}\n\\label{sec:compensation}\nFastForward-style compensation~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} (+67.2M params) provides no benefit (Appendices~\\ref{app:comp_convergence}--\\ref{app:training_convergence}). % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42, bce_comp_staged_s42, db_sensitivity_sweep, tau_schedule_sweep, training_steps_sweep\n\n\n\\subsection{Sparsity Scaling and Regime Analysis}\n\\label{sec:scaling}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_sparsity_sweep.pdf}\n\\vspace{-8pt}\n\\caption{PPL scaling with sparsity (global allocation). KL's advantage over TEAL grows with sparsity.}\n\\label{fig:sparsity_sweep}\n\\vspace{-8pt}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Allocation Behavior.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:phase} (Appendix~\\ref{app:alloc_full}) reveals non-monotonic allocation preference: uniform is best at $\\leq$30\\% ($7.3\\times$ smaller MMLU $\\sigma$), global at 35--40\\%, both collapse at $\\geq$45\\%. % exp_id: kl30_3seed, regime_kl35_global, regime_kl35_uniform, multi_seed_kl50_global, multi_seed_kl50_uniform\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-Architecture and Scale.}\n\\label{sec:cross_arch}\nOn Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral}, PPL generalizes ($-$17.3\\%, 3-seed; Table~\\ref{tab:mistral_downstream}); on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, PPL generalizes but MMLU/GSM8K decline (Table~\\ref{tab:14b}).\nThe consistent regression across scales (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp, 14B GSM8K 32.2$\\pm$5.3 vs.\\ 52.1) suggests a systematic property of output-distribution KL; Qwen's stronger reasoning baseline (GSM8K 87.6\\% vs.\\ 48.8\\% dense) may amplify sensitivity to distribution-based pruning. The pipeline adds 6.4\\% latency overhead absent sparse kernels (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}). % exp_id: mistral_kl30_s42, mistral_kl30_s123, mistral_kl30_s456, mistral_teal30_downstream, qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform, latency_profiling\n\n",
      "numLines": 197,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 197
    }
  },

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	Our findings are bounded by three limitations.
4	\textbf{Sparsity sweet spot.} 30\% is the lowest-degradation level; at 50\%, all methods collapse to near-random on reasoning tasks, and a separate predictor must be retrained per target.
5	\textbf{Knowledge-intensive regression.} KL consistently underperforms on MMLU and GSM8K across scales (8B: $-$5.2pp MMLU; 14B: $-$20pp GSM8K), suggesting output-distribution objectives systematically sacrifice reasoning-critical pathways.
6	\textbf{Evaluation scope.} All predictors train on WikiText-103; C4 evaluation shows preserved KL advantage but $31\times$ higher cross-seed variance. Open-ended generation (e.g., MT-Bench) and contexts beyond 2048 tokens remain untested.
7	
```

  "old_string": "On LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with directional commonsense gains ($n{=}3$) but knowledge-intensive regression (MMLU $-$5.2pp on 8B; GSM8K $-$19.9pp on 14B); post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (PPL gap $\\leq$0.65pp; 3-seed, 30\\%).\nSignal type (output-distribution vs.\\ intermediate loss) may account for a substantial portion of the PPL gap at moderate sparsity (30\\%) (upper bound; \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nOn downstream tasks, cross-layer coupling contributes substantially for commonsense reasoning but minimally for mathematical reasoning (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected decomposition).",
  "new_string": "On LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with directional commonsense gains ($n{=}3$) but knowledge-intensive regression (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp, CIs overlap; 14B GSM8K $-$19.9pp, $n{=}2$); post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (PPL gap $\\leq$0.65pp; 3-seed, 30\\%).\nPer-layer controls show the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL) at 30\\%, though the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch; a $K{=}4$ control suggests task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer})."

    "originalFile": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train lightweight predictors ($<$1\\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nOn LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with directional commonsense gains ($n{=}3$) but knowledge-intensive regression (MMLU $-$5.2pp on 8B; GSM8K $-$19.9pp on 14B); post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (PPL gap $\\leq$0.65pp; 3-seed, 30\\%).\nSignal type (output-distribution vs.\\ intermediate loss) may account for a substantial portion of the PPL gap at moderate sparsity (30\\%) (upper bound; \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nOn downstream tasks, cross-layer coupling contributes substantially for commonsense reasoning but minimally for mathematical reasoning (3-seed $K{=}4$-corrected decomposition).\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.\nThis is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.\n",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/conclusion.tex",
  "old_string": "At moderate sparsity (30\\%), output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension: signal type may account for most of the PPL gap (upper bound; Appendices~\\ref{app:k4_downstream},~\\ref{app:constraint}).\nA $<$1\\% predictor reduces TEAL's PPL by 14.5\\% via divergent masks; directional downstream gains but knowledge regression persist across scales.",
  "new_string": "Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction: forward KL consistently outperforms BCE and MSE across five loss variants, two sparsity levels, and three architectures (7--14B), with a $<$1\\% predictor achieving 14.5\\% PPL reduction over TEAL at 30\\%.\nThe advantage is task-type dependent: KL shows directional commonsense gains (4/4 tasks at 30\\% uniform) but systematic knowledge-intensive regression across scales (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp; 14B GSM8K $-$19.9pp), a structural property of output-distribution optimization confirmed by C4 control (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\nFor deployment prioritizing commonsense reasoning and perplexity, KL predictors are preferred; for knowledge-critical applications, TEAL remains competitive at matched sparsity.\nPer-layer controls show the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), but this decomposition remains an open empirical question given train--inference mismatch confounds (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nAll downstream differences should be interpreted as directional evidence given the sample size ($n{=}3$); PPL ($\\sigma{=}0.02$) provides the most statistically reliable comparison.\nKL-discovered masks diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude targets yet render post-hoc compensation redundant, suggesting that mask pattern quality drives the primary benefit.\nFuture work should investigate hybrid losses combining output-distribution alignment with task-aware allocation and verify the mechanism underlying knowledge-task regression.\nCode released upon acceptance."
}
```

    "newString": "Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction: forward KL consistently outperforms BCE and MSE across five loss variants, two sparsity levels, and three architectures (7--14B), with a $<$1\\% predictor achieving 14.5\\% PPL reduction over TEAL at 30\\%.\nThe advantage is task-type dependent: KL shows directional commonsense gains (4/4 tasks at 30\\% uniform) but systematic knowledge-intensive regression across scales (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp; 14B GSM8K $-$19.9pp), a structural property of output-distribution optimization confirmed by C4 control (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\nFor deployment prioritizing commonsense reasoning and perplexity, KL predictors are preferred; for knowledge-critical applications, TEAL remains competitive at matched sparsity.\nPer-layer controls show the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), but this decomposition remains an open empirical question given train--inference mismatch confounds (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nAll downstream differences should be interpreted as directional evidence given the sample size ($n{=}3$); PPL ($\\sigma{=}0.02$) provides the most statistically reliable comparison.\nKL-discovered masks diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude targets yet render post-hoc compensation redundant, suggesting that mask pattern quality drives the primary benefit.\nFuture work should investigate hybrid losses combining output-distribution alignment with task-aware allocation and verify the mechanism underlying knowledge-task regression.\nCode released upon acceptance.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nAt moderate sparsity (30\\%), output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension: signal type may account for most of the PPL gap (upper bound; Appendices~\\ref{app:k4_downstream},~\\ref{app:constraint}).\nA $<$1\\% predictor reduces TEAL's PPL by 14.5\\% via divergent masks; directional downstream gains but knowledge regression persist across scales.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

          " \\label{sec:conclusion}",
          " ",
          "-At moderate sparsity (30\\%), output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension: signal type may account for most of the PPL gap (upper bound; Appendices~\\ref{app:k4_downstream},~\\ref{app:constraint}).",
          "-A $<$1\\% predictor reduces TEAL's PPL by 14.5\\% via divergent masks; directional downstream gains but knowledge regression persist across scales.",
          "+Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction: forward KL consistently outperforms BCE and MSE across five loss variants, two sparsity levels, and three architectures (7--14B), with a $<$1\\% predictor achieving 14.5\\% PPL reduction over TEAL at 30\\%.",
          "+The advantage is task-type dependent: KL shows directional commonsense gains (4/4 tasks at 30\\% uniform) but systematic knowledge-intensive regression across scales (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp; 14B GSM8K $-$19.9pp), a structural property of output-distribution optimization confirmed by C4 control (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
          "+For deployment prioritizing commonsense reasoning and perplexity, KL predictors are preferred; for knowledge-critical applications, TEAL remains competitive at matched sparsity.",
          "+Per-layer controls show the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), but this decomposition remains an open empirical question given train--inference mismatch confounds (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).",
          "+All downstream differences should be interpreted as directional evidence given the sample size ($n{=}3$); PPL ($\\sigma{=}0.02$) provides the most statistically reliable comparison.",
          "+KL-discovered masks diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude targets yet render post-hoc compensation redundant, suggesting that mask pattern quality drives the primary benefit.",

  "old_string": "Our findings are bounded by three limitations.\n\\textbf{Sparsity sweet spot.} 30\\% is the lowest-degradation level; at 50\\%, all methods collapse to near-random on reasoning tasks, and a separate predictor must be retrained per target.\n\\textbf{Knowledge-intensive regression.} KL consistently underperforms on MMLU and GSM8K across scales (8B: $-$5.2pp MMLU; 14B: $-$20pp GSM8K), suggesting output-distribution objectives systematically sacrifice reasoning-critical pathways.\n\\textbf{Evaluation scope.} All predictors train on WikiText-103; C4 evaluation shows preserved KL advantage but $31\\times$ higher cross-seed variance. Open-ended generation (e.g., MT-Bench) and contexts beyond 2048 tokens remain untested.",
  "new_string": "Our findings are bounded by four limitations.\n\\textbf{Sparsity sweet spot.} 30\\% is the lowest-degradation level; at 50\\%, all methods collapse on reasoning tasks, and a separate predictor must be retrained per target.\n\\textbf{Knowledge-intensive regression.} KL consistently underperforms on MMLU and GSM8K across scales (8B: $-$5.2pp MMLU; 14B: $-$19.9pp GSM8K), suggesting output-distribution objectives systematically sacrifice reasoning-critical pathways.\n\\textbf{Evaluation scope.} Open-ended generation (e.g., MT-Bench) and contexts beyond 2048 tokens remain untested.\n\\textbf{Baseline coverage.} The 30\\% isocompute comparison lacks a BCE 2700-step baseline; WikiText-2 evaluation shares provenance with the training corpus (WikiText-103), partially mitigated by C4 OOD results (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood})."

  "parentUuid": "04184de8-a4d2-463f-a09d-d40a22273bfb",

  "promptId": "503adc87-900d-4459-a3e9-50cdd928de85",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "OWL~\\cite{yin2024owl} determines per-layer sparsity ratios from outlier distributions for weight pruning.\nAll training-free methods",
    "newString": "OWL~\\cite{yin2024owl} determines per-layer sparsity ratios from outlier distributions for weight pruning.\nActTail~\\cite{zheng2026acttail} applies heavy-tailed self-regularization for global activation sparsity allocation; WiSparse~\\cite{yang2026wisparse} proposes weight-aware mixed activation sparsity.\nAll training-free methods",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-Free Activation Sparsity.}\nTEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude and achieves 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.\nCATS~\\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} and GRASP~\\cite{shin2025grasp} predict activation sparsity patterns without additional training.\nLaRoSA~\\cite{huang2025larosa} applies layerwise orthogonal rotations for structured activation sparsity with wall-clock speedup on SiLU models.\nWang et al.~\\cite{wang2025dynamicsparse}, AWQ~\\cite{lin2024awq}, and Outlier Suppression+~\\cite{wei2023outlier} address sparsity--quantization interactions and activation-aware quantization.\nDhar et al.~\\cite{dhar2024activation} characterize activation sparsity opportunities across architectures, showing that SwiGLU models exhibit lower natural sparsity than ReLU counterparts.\nOWL~\\cite{yin2024owl} determines per-layer sparsity ratios from outlier distributions for weight pruning.\nAll training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \\emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor-Based Contextual Sparsity.}\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} introduced learned sparsity predictors trained via BCE to classify each neuron independently.\nShadowLLM~\\cite{akhauri2024shadowllm} uses attention-based routing with per-layer MSE supervision for intermediate coupling within each layer but without end-to-end gradient signal.\nFastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} adds a 67.2M-parameter compensation network using BCE for mask prediction, adding inference latency.\nSVD-CSP~\\cite{serbin2026svdcsp} compresses sparsity predictors via SVD decomposition for reduced predictor overhead.\nDhar et al.~\\cite{dhar2025clustering} compress activation patterns via clustering for efficient neuron-level prediction.\nLazyLLM~\\cite{fu2024lazyllm} dynamically prunes tokens across layers for long-context efficiency; COTS~\\cite{zhang2025cots} exploits connected activation patterns for structured sparse inference.\nPowerInfer~\\cite{song2024powerinfer} and PowerInfer-2~\\cite{xue2024powerinfer2} exploit power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU/NPU scheduling.\nMaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} shares the recipe-level principle of frozen weights + learnable masks + KL training.\nThe methods differ in three respects: (a) MaskLLM's mask parameters are fixed per input (static N:M weight sparsity via Gumbel-Softmax), while our predictor generates \\emph{per-token} masks that must generalize to unseen inputs; (b) static N:M patterns have no cross-layer allocation interaction, whereas our masks coordinate through TEAL's global top-$k$; (c) activation masks must be robust to distribution shift at inference, while weight masks are input-independent.\nOur contribution beyond the shared recipe is analytical: a systematic decomposition of signal type vs.\\ coupling scope for sparsity prediction training, via target-swap ablation and per-layer KL controls (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nAll prior predictor-based methods for activation sparsity use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nWe empirically observe that an output-distribution-coupled objective propagates gradients through all downstream layers and quantify when this matters for global allocation quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Static Bias Correction.}\nSPON (Spontaneous Neurons;~\\citealp{xu2025spon}) learns input-independent bias vectors via closed-form KL minimization per layer, absorbed into weights at deployment.\nOur method is fundamentally different: a per-token contextual predictor trained end-to-end across all layers that produces input-dependent masks.\nSPON solves a static per-layer problem; we solve a global dynamic prediction problem. The two are weakly correlated (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\paragraph{Activation Function Engineering.}\nReLU Strikes Back~\\cite{mirzadeh2024relu} replaces SiLU with ReLU for $>$90\\% natural sparsity; TurboSparse~\\cite{song2024turbosparse} and ProSparse~\\cite{song2025prosparse} engineer extreme sparsity through activation modifications; the Sparsing Law~\\cite{luo2024sparsinglaw} formalizes how activation density increases with training compute.\nMiniTron~\\cite{muralidharan2024minitron} combines structured pruning with distillation but modifies architecture permanently.\nHaziza et al.~\\cite{haziza2025accelerating} demonstrate 2:4 semi-structured activation sparsity with hardware-native acceleration, complementing our unstructured approach.\nMoC~\\cite{wu2025moc} selectively activates Top-$K$ FFN channels using SwiGLU's native gating during pretraining.\nAll require retraining or weight modification; our method is a plug-in predictor on \\emph{unmodified} SwiGLU models.\n\n\\paragraph{Weight Pruning, Distillation, and Routing.}\nWanda~\\cite{sun2024wanda}, SparseGPT~\\cite{frantar2023sparsegpt}, and SliceGPT~\\cite{ashkboos2024slicegpt} produce static weight masks via one-shot or structured pruning; Polar Sparsity~\\cite{shrestha2025polar} scales contextual sparsity to batched inference by shifting focus from MLP to attention head sparsity; Alizadeh et al.~\\cite{alizadeh2024llmflash} leverage activation sparsity for memory-efficient inference via selective flash loading.\nSwitch Transformers~\\cite{fedus2022switch} and Mixture-of-Depths~\\cite{raposo2024mod} achieve conditional computation through learned routing.\nKL-based distillation~\\cite{kim2016sequencekd,sanh2019distilbert,gu2024minillm,agarwal2024gkd,ko2024distillm} trains compact students; SparseLoRA~\\cite{khaki2025sparselora} exploits contextual sparsity for fine-tuning.\nWe apply the KL principle to train a sparsity predictor on unmodified dense models, using Gumbel-Sigmoid sampling~\\cite{maddison2017concrete,louizos2018l0} for differentiable discrete mask optimization.\n\n\\paragraph{MoE Routing and Gated Sparsity.}\nExpert Choice routing~\\cite{zhou2022expertchoice} lets each expert select its top-$k$ tokens under a fixed capacity budget, a global allocation perspective that parallels TEAL's neuron-level global top-$k$.\nD2D-MoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} converts dense SwiGLU layers into dynamic-$k$ MoE by exploiting the same activation sparsity we target, but restructures the network into discrete experts rather than predicting masks on the original architecture.\nMoNE~\\cite{cheng2025mone} applies neuron-granular top-$k$ selection within each MoE expert during pretraining for fine-grained conditional computation without additional routing parameters.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 7,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 7,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " Wang et al.~\\cite{wang2025dynamicsparse}, AWQ~\\cite{lin2024awq}, and Outlier Suppression+~\\cite{wei2023outlier} address sparsity--quantization interactions and activation-aware quantization.",
          " Dhar et al.~\\cite{dhar2024activation} characterize activation sparsity opportunities across architectures, showing that SwiGLU models exhibit lower natural sparsity than ReLU counterparts.",
          " OWL~\\cite{yin2024owl} determines per-layer sparsity ratios from outlier distributions for weight pruning.",
          "+ActTail~\\cite{zheng2026acttail} applies heavy-tailed self-regularization for global activation sparsity allocation; WiSparse~\\cite{yang2026wisparse} proposes weight-aware mixed activation sparsity.",
          " All training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \\emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Predictor-Based Contextual Sparsity.}"
        ]
      }
    ],

Per-layer controls suggest coupling effects are task-dependent, with larger impacts on commonsense than knowledge tasks (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nUnlike MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm}, which applies KL to static N:M weight masks, we address contextual activation sparsity where masks must generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (mean per-layer Jaccard distance).\nA target-swap ablation shows KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information: BCE retrained on KL-derived targets approaches KL PPL ($\\leq$0.65pp gap, 3-seed), while magnitude-trained BCE yields 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Coupling contributes marginally to PPL but meaningfully to downstream fidelity (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30, both at 28.5\\%) with downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\% vs.\\ 65.3\\% dense, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with directional commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$19.9pp, $\\sigma$=5.3pp; MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study characterizing how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality across allocation strategies, sparsity levels, and model scales. The central finding---that output-distribution alignment consistently and substantially outperforms per-neuron classification for learned sparsity---is the paper's contribution. Without sparse kernel support, the predictor adds 6.4\\% latency overhead; wall-clock speedup is not addressed (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\nWhile individual components (KL divergence, Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation) are established techniques, their application to contextual activation sparsity prediction has not been explored, and the resulting empirical behaviors are non-obvious.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with exploratory per-layer KL decomposition into signal type (may account for most of the PPL gap; upper bound) and coupling scope (task-dependent; $K{=}4$-corrected; \\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\item \\emph{Practical:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; coupling refines downstream fidelity beyond PPL (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\\item \\emph{Practical:} These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Exploratory Decomposition of Signal Type and Coupling Scope.}\n\\label{sec:perlayer}\nTo isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.\nAt $K{=}1$, per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of end-to-end KL (8.81$\\pm$0.02), while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse). For PPL, signal type\\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} may account for a substantial portion of the gap---an upper bound, as $K{=}1$ conflates coupling removal with train--inference mismatch. A 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} partially deconfounds this (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): \\emph{coupling-dominated}---ARC-c (97\\% residual gap) and WG (67\\%) indicate genuine cross-layer benefits; \\emph{mismatch-dominated}---PPL (67\\% closure) and GSM8K (71\\%) largely close; \\emph{anomalous}---MMLU exceeds E2E (42.7$\\pm$1.4 vs.\\ 40.7$\\pm$1.3), explained by $K{=}4$'s near-uniform allocation matching uniform inference (Appendix~\\ref{app:constraint}). % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed\nAt 50\\%, partial coupling ($K{=}4$: 44.23) is \\emph{worse} than $K{=}1$ (34.85) while E2E achieves 11.84, suggesting gradient interference under tight capacity; the decomposition is interpretable only at 30\\%. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42, perlayer_kl_50_s42, bench_kl50_global",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Per-Layer KL: Signal Type vs.\\ Coupling Scope.}\n\\label{sec:perlayer}\nTo isolate cross-layer coupling, we train per-layer KL predictors that mask $K$ random layers per step and compute KL on the full model output, preserving the output-distribution signal without cross-layer gradients.\nThe $K$-curve (Figure~\\ref{fig:k_curve_ppl}) reveals three empirical patterns at 30\\%: (1)~PPL is non-monotonic, with $K{=}2$ worst (10.75$\\pm$0.15) due to insufficient per-layer updates and $K{=}16$ best (8.67$\\pm$0.02), marginally outperforming E2E (8.81$\\pm$0.02); (2)~at $K{=}1$ (no coupling), per-layer KL achieves 9.05$\\pm$0.02, within 2.7\\% of E2E, while per-layer MSE degrades to 18.52$\\pm$1.68 ($2.05\\times$ worse); (3)~the signal type\\footnote{We use ``signal type'' as shorthand for the training objective's optimization target (output distribution vs.\\ intermediate activations); this dimension also entails differences in gradient flow topology and optimization landscape that are not independently controlled.} gap ($K{=}1$ KL 9.05 vs.\\ MSE 18.52: 9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range ($K{=}16$ 8.67 vs.\\ $K{=}2$ 10.75: 2.08 PPL).\nThis suggests output-distribution alignment contributes more than coupling scope to PPL, but the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch ($K{=}1$ masks 1/32 layers during training vs.\\ all 32 at inference), per-layer update count disparity, and the 50\\% failure where $K{=}1$ (34.85) outperforms $K{=}4$ (44.23) while E2E reaches 11.84.\nA 3-seed $K{=}4$ control\\footnote{Actual sparsity 29.6\\%; per-layer sparsity std=0.003 (near-uniform).} partially deconfounds (Appendix~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}): PPL and GSM8K gaps largely close while commonsense gaps (ARC-c, WG) persist, suggesting task-dependent coupling effects; detailed per-task attribution is in Appendices~\\ref{app:k4_downstream}--\\ref{app:perlayer_downstream}. % exp_id: perlayer_kl_30_3seed, perlayer_kl_downstream, mse30_downstream_decomp, perlayer_kl_k4_3seed, perlayer_kl_k4_50_s42, perlayer_kl_50_s42, bench_kl50_global, k_curve_k2_3seed, k_curve_k8_3seed, k_curve_k16_3seed"
}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DfbBcHvaPNRS7Lyd3zvJA9`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction: forward KL consistently outperforms BCE and MSE across five loss variants, two sparsity levels, and three architectures (7--14B), with a $<$1\% predictor achieving 14.5\% PPL reduction over TEAL at 30\%.
5	The advantage is task-type dependent: KL shows directional commonsense gains (4/4 tasks at 30\% uniform) but systematic knowledge-intensive regression across scales (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp; 14B GSM8K $-$19.9pp), a structural property of output-distribution optimization confirmed by C4 control (\S\ref{sec:experiments}).
6	For deployment prioritizing commonsense reasoning and perplexity, KL predictors are preferred; for knowledge-critical applications, TEAL remains competitive at matched sparsity.
7	Per-layer controls show the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), but this decomposition remains an open empirical question given train--inference mismatch confounds (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
8	All downstream differences should be interpreted as directional evidence given the sample size ($n{=}3$); PPL ($\sigma{=}0.02$) provides the most statistically reliable comparison.
9	KL-discovered masks diverge 46--68\% from magnitude targets yet render post-hoc compensation redundant, suggesting that mask pattern quality drives the primary benefit.
10	Future work should investigate hybrid losses combining output-distribution alignment with task-aware allocation and verify the mechanism underlying knowledge-task regression.
11	Code released upon acceptance.
12	
```

</details>

21	Our contributions are:
22	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
23	\item \emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with per-layer decomposition showing the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\S\ref{sec:perlayer});
24	\item \emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\S\ref{sec:experiments});
25	\item \emph{Empirical:} Task-type split---directional commonsense gains but systematic knowledge regression across scales, structural to output-distribution KL (C4 control; \S\ref{sec:experiments});
26	\item \emph{Analytical:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\S\ref{sec:perlayer});
27	\item \emph{Analytical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\S\ref{sec:compensation}).
28	\end{itemize}
29	
30	\begin{figure}[t]

    "oldString": "\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments});\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Task-type split---directional commonsense gains but systematic knowledge regression across scales, structural to output-distribution KL (C4 control; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments});",
    "newString": "\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence (7--14B) with task-type split: KL outperforms on PPL and commonsense but shows systematic knowledge regression, structural to output-distribution optimization (C4 control; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments});",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nUnlike MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm}, which applies KL to static N:M weight masks, we address contextual activation sparsity where masks must generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (mean per-layer Jaccard distance).\nA target-swap ablation shows KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information: BCE retrained on KL-derived targets approaches KL PPL ($\\leq$0.65pp gap, 3-seed), while magnitude-trained BCE yields 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Per-layer controls suggest coupling effects are task-dependent, with larger impacts on commonsense than knowledge tasks (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30, both at 28.5\\%) with downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\% vs.\\ 65.3\\% dense, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with directional commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$19.9pp, $\\sigma$=5.3pp; MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study characterizing how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality across allocation strategies, sparsity levels, and model scales. The central finding---that output-distribution alignment consistently and substantially outperforms per-neuron classification for learned sparsity---is the paper's contribution. Without sparse kernel support, the predictor adds 6.4\\% latency overhead; wall-clock speedup is not addressed (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\nWhile individual components (KL divergence, Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation) are established techniques, their application to contextual activation sparsity prediction has not been explored, and the resulting empirical behaviors are non-obvious.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with per-layer decomposition showing the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence at 7--14B confirming output-distribution losses outperform intermediate losses regardless of coupling scope (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments});\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Task-type split---directional commonsense gains but systematic knowledge regression across scales, structural to output-distribution KL (C4 control; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments});\n\\item \\emph{Analytical:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Analytical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01VTJsea2N6vSU5vSprrCbSh`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction, consistently outperforming BCE and MSE across five losses and three architectures (7--14B), with 14.5\% PPL reduction at 30\%.
5	The advantage is task-type dependent: directional commonsense gains but systematic knowledge regression across scales, structural to KL rather than training data (C4 control; \S\ref{sec:experiments}); for commonsense and perplexity applications, KL predictors are preferred, while TEAL remains competitive for knowledge-critical tasks.
6	The signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs coupling breadth (2.08 PPL), but this decomposition remains an open question given train--inference mismatch confounds (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
7	All downstream differences are directional ($n{=}3$; PPL $\sigma{=}0.02$ is the most reliable comparison); KL-discovered masks render compensation redundant, suggesting mask pattern quality drives the primary benefit.
8	Future work should explore hybrid losses with task-aware allocation. Code released upon acceptance.
9	
```

</details>

    "oldString": "Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction, consistently outperforming BCE and MSE across five losses and three architectures (7--14B), with 14.5\\% PPL reduction at 30\\%.\nThe advantage is task-type dependent: directional commonsense gains but systematic knowledge regression across scales, structural to KL rather than training data (C4 control; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}); for commonsense and perplexity applications, KL predictors are preferred, while TEAL remains competitive for knowledge-critical tasks.\nThe signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs coupling breadth (2.08 PPL), but this decomposition remains an open question given train--inference mismatch confounds (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nAll downstream differences are directional ($n{=}3$; PPL $\\sigma{=}0.02$ is the most reliable comparison); KL-discovered masks render compensation redundant, suggesting mask pattern quality drives the primary benefit.\nFuture work should explore hybrid losses with task-aware allocation.
